  4. The minimum height of a plane mirror to enable a person to see full image is 1/2 of his height. According to the principle of reflection, the angle of incidence is equal to angle of reflection. When the ray of light travels from bottom to top of a person and reaches his eyes then he is able to see the clear image. The ray from the foot strikes the mirror at its bottom and reaches his eye after reflecting from the mirror. Thus, the height of the mirror must equal to half the height of the person.×4

Chapter 1

Measurement/Unit

Comprehensive short notes on Measurement/Unit (Physics) with key concepts, definitions, and real UPPSC previous year questions.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Acceleration is the rate of change of velocity of an object, with respect to time. The SI unit of acceleration is metre per second square (m...PCS Pre ’052 papers
  2. Celsius is a scale and unit of measurement for temperature. Heat is measured in Calories. Nautical mile is used to measure distance at sea. ...PCS Mains (Spl) ’042 papers
  3. The parsec (symbol: pc) is a unit of length used to measure the large distances to astronomical objects outside the Solar System, approximat...RO/ARO Pre ’231 paper

Measurement (मापन)/Unit (मात्रक): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • A unit (मात्रक) of measurement (मापन) is a definite magnitude of a quantity, defined and adopted by convention or by law.
  • In physics, power (शक्ति) is the rate of doing work (कार्य) or of transferring heat (ऊष्मा) or electrical energy (ऊर्जा) i.e. the amount of energy transferred or converted per unit (मात्रक) time. Power (P) = Time (t)Work (W) The SI unit of power is watt, which is equal to joule per second. Power is always represented in watt (W) or kilowatt (kW).
  • When a force (बल) acts to move an object, then work (कार्य) done by the force is equivalent to the product of force and displacement in the direction of force . It is a scalar quantity. The SI unit (मात्रक) of work is Newton metre, which is also called as joule. Joule is also the unit of Energy (ऊर्जा).
  • Unit (मात्रक) of radioactivity (रेडियोधर्मिता) is defined as the activity of a quantity of radioactive material where one decay takes place per second. The SI unit of radioactivity is becquerel (Bq) and this term is named after Henri Becquerel. Curie (Ci) is the old U.S. unit (non-S.I. unit) of radioactivity. Other pairs are correctly matched.
  • Light (प्रकाश)-year i s a unit (मात्रक) of astronomical distance equivalent to the distance that light travels in one year, which is 9.461 × 1015 metres.

Measurement (मापन)/Unit (मात्रक): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • It is used as a standard for measurement (मापन) of the same kind of quantity.
  • The parsec (symbol: pc) is a unit (मात्रक) of length used to measure the large distances to astronomical objects outside the Solar System, approximately equal to 3.262 light (प्रकाश)-years or ~ 206265 astronomical units (au), i.e. 30.9 trillion kilometres (19.2 trillion miles). A parsec is the distance from the Sun to an astronomical object that has a parallax angle...
  • A parsec is a unit (मात्रक) of length used to measure the astronomically large distances of objects beyond our solar system. 1 Parsec = 3.0857 × 1016 metres 1 Light (प्रकाश)-year = 9.461 × 1015 metres So, 1 Parsec = 3.262 light-years, Now according to options, only (b) is close to the exact answer.
  • 1 Centimetre = 0.39 inch, so we can multiply centimetre by 0.39 to convert it to 'inches'.
  • 1 nanometre = 1.0 × 10-9 metre Since, 1 metre = 100 cm So, 10-9 metre = 102 × 10-9 cm = 10-7 cm
  • 1 metre = 109 nanometres; 1 nanometre = 10–9 metre.
  • Megawatt is the measuring unit (मात्रक) of power (शक्ति), which is generated in Power station or Power Plant. 1 Megawatt is equal to 106 (1 million) watt.

Measurement (मापन)/Unit (मात्रक): Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Any other quantity of that kind can be expressed as a multiple of the unit (मात्रक) of measurement (मापन).
  • Acceleration (त्वरण) is the rate of change of velocity (वेग) of an object, with respect to time. The SI unit (मात्रक) of acceleration is metre per second square (m/s 2). The SI unit of force (बल) is newton (N). The SI unit of impulse is newton second (Ns). The SI unit of work (कार्य) is joule.
  • Pascal is the unit (मात्रक) of pressure (दाब) or stress in the International System of Units (SI). Dyne is the unit of force (बल) in CGS system. Clearly, option (d) is not correctly matched.
  • The SI unit (मात्रक) of power (शक्ति) is watt (W), which is equal to one joule per second. Knot is the unit of measuring of speed (चाल) of a ship. Nautical mile is a unit of distance used by navigators in the sea. Calorie is a unit of measuring heat (ऊष्मा) and energy (ऊर्जा). Thus option (c) correctly matched.
  • Joule is the SI unit (मात्रक) of work (कार्य). Ampere, watt and volt are the units of current, power (शक्ति) and electric potential respectively.
  • Light (प्रकाश) year is not a unit (मात्रक) of measuring time, but a unit of measuring distance. The remaining pairs are correctly matched.
  • In fluid mechanics, the Mach number is a dimensionless quantity representing the ratio of the speed (चाल) of a body to the speed of sound (ध्वनि) in the surrounding medium. Supersonic travell is a rate of an object that exceeds the speed of sound (Mach 1). So Mach is used to represent the high speed. Mechanics: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Mechanics : Mechanics is that area of science concerned with the behaviour of physical bodies when subjected to force (बल) or displacements and the subsequent effects of the bodies on their environment.
  • Weight (भार) : Weight is the force (बल) exerted on a body by gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण)/ force with which the Earth attracts it.
  • Mass (द्रव्यमान) : Mass represents the amount of matter (measure of inertia) in a particle or an object. Mass remains the same everywhere (in Classical Mechanics). It is denoted by 'm'. Difference between Weight (भार) and Mass S.N. Weight (W) Mass (m) 1. 2. 3. 4. Gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) force (बल) of Earth It is a vector (वाहक) quantity. Weight varies according to places. Its SI unit (मात्रक) is newton (N). A measurement (मापन) of the amount of matter something contains It is a scalar quantity. It is constant at every place. Its SI unit is kilogram (kg). Distance & Displacement :
  • In daily language Distance & Displacement are used in the same sense but in Physics both have a different meaning.
  • The length of a specific path travelled between two points by an object is distance.
  • Displacement is the shortest distance measured from the initial to the final position of an object (along a straight line) and has direction from initial to final position.
  • Travelled distance has only magnitude, while displacement has magnitude along with the direction. It is clear that distance is a scalar quantity and displacement is a vector (वाहक) quantity. In a course of motion, the travelled distance by an object is not zero, but the final displacement may be zero. Displacement of an object will be zero when the moving object comes to its initial point. Speed (चाल) & Velocity (वेग) :
  • Speed (चाल) is the distance travelled per unit (मात्रक) of time. The average speed of an object is obtained by dividing the total distance by the total time taken. ∴ Average speed = Totalt imet akenTotalt ravelledd istance It is a scalar quantity and its SI unit is metre/second.
  • The reading of the balance increases if the person inhales deeply. One litre of air weighs about 1.225 g. A maximal 4.8 litre breath (average vital capacity for males) weighs approximately 5.88g. Inhaled air will add to the mass (द्रव्यमान) of the man and thus his weight (भार) goes up slightly.
  • The scalar quantity is defined as the quantity that has only got magnitude, but not directions whereas, vector (वाहक) quantity comprises of both magnitude and directions. Displacement, velocity (वेग) and force (बल) are the examples of vector quantity while volume is a scalar quantity.

  • All the planets including the Earth, move around the Sun in their orbits.
  • A natural satellite (moon) is a naturally formed celestial body in space that orbits a planet or a dwarf planet; even some asteroids can have moons.

  • Earth has one natural satellite.
  • Mars has two small satellites.
  • Jupiter has 101 known satellites with confirmed orbits (As of 16 March, 2026).
  • Saturn has maximum numbers of known satellites with confirmed orbits i.e. 285 (As of 16 March, 2026).
  • As of 16 March, 2026, Uranus has 29 satellites, Neptune has 16 satellites, and dwarf planet Pluto has 5 satellites. Kepler's Laws of Planetary Motion :

Mechanics: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • The second law clears that for the same planet, it moves faster when it is nearer the Sun and slower when it is farther from the Sun. Third Law –
  • The square of the orbital period of a planet is directly proportional to the cube of the semi-major axis of its orbit.
  • Kepler's third law states P2 ∝ a3. That means, for bodies orbiting the Sun, knowing the orbital period (P) allows estimation of the semi-major axis (a) of its orbit. It means that if you know the planet orbit (P = how long it takes the planet to move around the Sun) then you can determine that planet distance from the Sun (a = the semi-major axis of the planet's orbit). Newton's Law of Gravitation :
  • Newton proposed that all the bodies of the Universe attract each other by a force (बल) called gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) force.
  • Newton propounded following law about gravitation – "The attracting (gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण)) force (बल) between two objects is directly proportional to the product of their masses and inversely proportional to the square of the distance between their centres."
  • Newton's Law of Gravitation can be expressed mathematically as follows : If two objects whose masses are m1 and m2 respectively, and they are positioned at a distance r, then the attraction of force (बल) between them will be /c181 where G is the constant of proportionality known as universal gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) constant.
  • If the objects mass (द्रव्यमान) is unit (मात्रक) (m1 & m2 both 1 kg.) and distance between them is unit (r = 1 m), then – F = G It means that numerical value of gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) constant (G) equals the force (बल) of attraction between two objects with unit mass and positioned at a unit distance.
  • In SI System its value is 6.67430 × 10 –11 newton.metre2 per kg2 (N.m2.kg–2/m3.kg–1.s–2)

In other words, once you start an object moving, the friction decreases from the static friction holding the object in place. You have seen this in trying to slide a heavy box across the floor. Motion under Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • The Motion of Planets :
  • All the planets including the Earth, move around the Sun in their orbits.
  • A natural satellite (moon) is a naturally formed celestial body in space that orbits a planet or a dwarf planet; even some asteroids can have moons.
  • Planets Mercury and Venus have no natural satellites.
  • Earth has one natural satellite.
  • Mars has two small satellites.
  • Jupiter has 101 known satellites with confirmed orbits (As of 16 March, 2026).
  • Saturn has maximum numbers of known satellites with confirmed orbits i.e. 285 (As of 16 March, 2026).
  • In space, astronauts can't stand erect because there is no gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) or microgravity. In this condition people or objects appear to be weightless. Microgravity affects the human body in several ways. For example muscles and bones can become weaker without gravity.
  • If an apple is released from an orbiting spaceship, it will move along with the spaceship at the same speed (चाल).

Motion under Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • If the objects mass (द्रव्यमान) is unit (मात्रक) (m1 & m2 both 1 kg.) and distance between them is unit (r = 1 m), then – F = G It means that numerical value of gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) constant (G) equals the force (बल) of attraction between two objects with unit mass and positioned at a unit distance.
  • In SI System its value is 6.67430 × 10 –11 newton.metre2 per kg2 (N.m2.kg–2/m3.kg–1.s–2)
  • The value of G was first determined experimentally by Henry Cavendish. Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) and Gravitational acceleration (त्वरण) of Earth :
  • Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) is attraction force (बल) by which the Earth attracts objects towards its centre.
  • It is a peculiar form of gravitation.
  • When an object is thrown or dropped freely, it falls towards the Earth due to the gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) of Earth and its falling rate is continuously increasing due to the production of acceleration (त्वरण), which is known as gravitational acceleration. It is represented by 'g'. Its unit (मात्रक) is metre/second2. Relation between 'g' & 'G' :
  • Gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) acceleration (त्वरण) of Earth where, G = Gravitational constant Me = Mass (द्रव्यमान) of Earth Re = Radius of Earth
  • Value of 'g' does not depend upon the mass (द्रव्यमान) of the object.
  • Both balls A and B will reach the ground at the same time. Be- cause both balls are thrown from the equal height of 20 metre. Motion under Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण): Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • If the gravitational (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) acceleration (त्वरण) of Earth is 'g' then, g = Re2 eGM ⇒ G Me = g Re2 ∴ Ve = R2g Re e2 Taking gravitational acceleration (g) = 9.8 m/second2 and Mean Radius of Earth = 6.37 × 106 metre By placing these values in equation – V e = = 11.2 × 103 metre/second = 11.2 km/second
  • It is clear that if any object is thrown from the surface of the Earth at the minimum speed (चाल) of 11.2 km/second, (in ideal no air resistance (प्रतिरोध)), that object will move in space and never comes back to the Earth. Circular Motion and Escape Velocity (वेग) relation :
  • If the circular orbital speed (चाल) of a satellite revolving nearby to Earth is Vo and the escape velocity (वेग) (escape speed) of an object thrown from Earth's surface is Ve, then– V e = Vo
  • It is clear that at the same distance from Earth's centre a satellite revolving nearby to Earth, if by any reason, its speed (चाल) exceeds by at least (or about 1.414) times its circular orbital speed (idealised), it will escape into space, leaving its orbit (neglecting drag and other forces). Periodic Motion, Oscillatory Motion and SHM :
  • Periodic motion : motion that repeats itself after equal intervals of time.
  • Oscillatory motion : to-and-fro motion about a mean (equilibrium) position.
  • Simple Harmonic Motion (SHM) : an oscillatory motion in which the restoring force (बल) (or acceleration (त्वरण)) is directly proportional to the displacement from the mean position and is directed towards the mean position. Examples: a mass (द्रव्यमान) attached to a spring; a simple pendulum for small angular displacement. Simple Pendulum –
  • An ideal simple pendulum consists of a point mass (द्रव्यमान) (bob) suspended by a light (प्रकाश), inextensible string from a fixed support, oscillating in a vertical plane with negligible friction/air resistance (प्रतिरोध).
  • Centripetal force (बल) is a real force that counteracts the centrifugal force and prevents the object from 'flying out,' keeping it moving instead with a uniform speed (चाल) along a circular or elliptical path. Gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) is the centripetal force that keeps planets moving around the sun and satellites moving around planets.

Physical Properties of Matter: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Matter around us exists in three different states—(i) solid, (ii) liquid, and (iii) gas.
  • The three states of matter show characteristic mechanical properties (many properties overlap across states):
  • Moon has no atmosphere because the value of acceleration (त्वरण) due to gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) 'g' on the surface of the Moon is small. Therefore, the value of escape velocity (वेग) on the surface of the Moon is small. The value of root mean square (RMS) velocity of the molecules of different gases is much above the value of escape velocity on the Moon. That is why all the mol...
  • NASA made it clear by his experiment that gravitation is must for lighting up a candle. In the case of microgravity in the spacecraft, we are able to light (प्रकाश) up the flame but it is not possible in an actual vacuum. In fact, the flickering frequency (आवृत्ति) of a flame is proportional to the square root of the acceleration (त्वरण) due to gravity (गुरुत्वाकर्षण) to the diameter of the...
  • The Young's modulus (E), also known as modulus of elasticity, is a property of the material that tells us how easily it can stretch and deform and is defined as the ratio of tensile stress to tensile strain. Where stress is the amount of force (बल) applied per unit (मात्रक) area and strain is extension per unit length. The Young's modulus for steel is much more ...
  • The shape of a drop of rain is constrained by the surface tension (पृष्ठ तनाव), which tries to give it the shape for which the surface area is minimum for the given volume. The spherical shape has the minimum surface area. That's why rain drops acquire spherical shape.
  • Oil spreads on water surface because the surface tension (पृष्ठ तनाव) of oil is less than water. Therefore, when oil is poured over water, the greater value of surface tension of water pulls oil in all directions, and as such it spreads on the water.
  • Soap bubbles are large because when soap dissolved in water its surface tension (पृष्ठ तनाव) is reduced. The pressure (दाब) inside a soap bubble is more than atmospheric pressure (वायुमंडलीय दाब).

Physical Properties of Matter: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Liquids that wet glass (acute contact angle) show capillary rise while liquid that is not wet glass (e.g. : mercury in glass) sho capillary depression.
  • Visible light (प्रकाश) is electromagnetic radiation (विकिरण) detectable by the human eye. It lies between infrared (longer wavelengths) and ultraviolet (shorter wavelengths).
  • When some charge is placed on a soap bubble, the bubble will expand and its radius increases because the charge will distribute itself evenly over the surface of the bubble, and the repulsion between the same charges will cause it to expand until the surface tension (पृष्ठ तनाव) can bring it to equilibrium. This will happen to both positive and negatively charg...
  • The fountain pen leak at high altitudes. It is because of low air pressure (दाब). The ink inside the fountain pen has high pressure compared to the low atmospheric pressure (वायुमंडलीय दाब) outside at high altitude, so ink flows out of the pen.
  • A sudden fall in barometer reading indicates that the weather will be stormy because atmospheric pressure (वायुमंडलीय दाब) (दाब) decreases rapidly at the onset of a storm.
  • In a streamlined motion, the speed (चाल) of layers of liquid is lowest near the pipe. As moved away from the pipe, the motion of layers gradually increases.

  • In a homogeneous medium, under geometrical optics conditions (objects and apertures >> wavelength (तरंगदैर्ध्य)), light (प्रकाश) travels approximately in straight lines; light can also diffract (spread/bend) around obstacles and apertures.

  • According to the Archimedes' principle of flotation, object appears to be heavier in the air than inside the water or they appear to be less heavy in the water. It is because water exerts an upward force (बल) on the objects immersed in it. This reduction (अपचयन) is equal to the weight (भार) of the fluid that body displaces.
  • Weight (भार) of balloon in water = Weight – Upthrust = 200 – 200 = 0 Hence, the balloon will be weightless in water.

Optics: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Liquids that wet glass (acute contact angle) show capillary rise while liquid that is not wet glass (e.g. : mercury in glass) sho capillary depression.
  • Visible light (प्रकाश) is electromagnetic radiation (विकिरण) detectable by the human eye. It lies between infrared (longer wavelengths) and ultraviolet (shorter wavelengths).
  • Wavelength (तरंगदैर्ध्य) range (approx.): about 380–700 nm (≈ 3.8×10–7 – 7.0×10–7 m). Some texts extend the upper limit to about 760 nm depending on convention.
  • Light (प्रकाश) is perceived only when light from a source/object enters the eyes. Objects are seen because they emit light or reflect/scatter incident light into our eyes.
  • In a homogeneous medium, under geometrical optics conditions (objects and apertures >> wavelength (तरंगदैर्ध्य)), light (प्रकाश) travels approximately in straight lines; light can also diffract (spread/bend) around obstacles and apertures.
  • Light (प्रकाश) can travel through a vacuum (sound (ध्वनि) waves cannot).
  • Light (प्रकाश) shows wave–particle duality (wave-like and particle- like behaviour).
  • Thus, light (प्रकाश) has dual nature : (a) Particle nature – behaves in the form of particles. (b) Wave nature – behaves in the form of waves. Speed (चाल) of light in different media (approx.)   • It is easier to swim in sea water than in a river because, the sea water contains salts which increase the density (घनत्व) of water and also increase its upthrust so, the chances of sinking get reduced and one can easily swim in such water.
  • The upthrust force (बल) or buoyant force in Hydrogen will be less than other matters because it is the lightest gas. So, option (c) is the correct answer.

Optics: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • The angle of incidence (i) is the angle between the incident ray and the normal.
  • The angle of refraction (अपवर्तन) (r) is the angle between the refracted ray and the normal in the second medium.
  • When light (प्रकाश) goes from an optically rarer medium (lower refractive index) to an optically denser medium (higher refractive index) it bends towards the normal; from optically denser to optically rarer it bends away from the normal. Refracted Ray i r Optically rarer medium Optically denser medium Normal line i r Optically rarer medium Optically denser medium Normal line Examples of Refractive Phenomenon : (i) An rod/pencil partially immersed in water appears bent/ displaced at the air-water interface when observed from outside. (ii) A coin in water appears raised above its actual position. (iii) Twinkling of stars (due to atmospheric refraction (अपवर्तन)). Refraction of light in a Prism :
  • A prism is a transparent refracting medium bounded by to non-parallel plane faces inclined at an angle (angle of prism).
  • Prisms are commonly made of glass (also other transparent materials are used).
  • In the above figure, the light (प्रकाश) ray AB, after falling on the first refracting surface PQ of the prism, is refracted along BC direction inside the prism, bending towards normal line EBF. Refracted ray BC at second refracting surface PR emerges into air along CD direction bending away from the normal line GCF. Thus, ABCD is the path of light in a Prism. Dispersion (विक्षेपण) of Light :
  • White light (प्रकाश) contains all visible wavelengths and is commonly represented as seven colours: Violet, Indigo, Blue, Green, Yellow, Orange and Red (VIBGYOR).
  • The splitting/spreading of white light (प्रकाश) into its constituent colours (wavelengths) is called dispersion (विक्षेपण).
  • The optical fibre is a very thin strand of glass (silica) or plastic cable for transmitting light (प्रकाश) from one point to another. It works on the principle of total internal reflection (पूर्ण आंतरिक परावर्तन) (परावर्तन). There is no loss of signal through an optical fibre.
  • Optical fibres are very thin, flexible and transparent cables made up of glass (silica) or plastic cable. They work (कार्य) on the principle of total internal reflection (पूर्ण आंतरिक परावर्तन) (परावर्तन). In an optical fibre communication system, power (शक्ति) consumption is extremely low.   • When light (प्रकाश) falls on the surface it may be absorbed, transmitted or reflected.
  • Reflection (परावर्तन) is when light (प्रकाश) bounces off an object.
  • Smooth, polished surfaces give specular (regular) reflection (परावर्तन), while rough surfaces give diffuse reflection (reflected rays spread in many directions).
  • Silver is the better reflector of light (प्रकाश). Mirror :
  • A smooth well polished reflecting surface is called the mirror.

Optics: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • Convex mirrors have outward curved reflective surface.
  • It is used as rear-view side mirrors in vehicles. Lens :
  • A lens is a transparent (usually homogeneous) material bounded by two surfaces, of which one or both are spherical; the other may be plane.
  • Curved surface may be spherical, cylindrical or elliptical, but these are mostly spherical.
  • Lens is of two types- (i) Convex Lens (ii) Concave Lens Convex Lens –
  • Convex lens is thick in center but thin at corner.
  • Convex lens is also known as a converging lens because it converges the parallel light (प्रकाश) rays at one point. Its focal length is positive.
  • A convex (converging) lens forms a real, inverted image when the object is beyond the focus; if the object lies between the focus and the optical centre, the image is virtual, erect and enlarged (e.g., magnifying glass).
  • When an object is placed in between two parallel plane mirrors, then the number of images formed will be infinite. Each image will be the result of an image. When two plane mirrors are inclined at an angle of θ, then the number of images formed by the object between them is (If the value of 360°θ is even), or 360°ibl (If the value of 360°θ is odd).

Heat (ऊष्मा) and Thermodynamics: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Heat (ऊष्मा) is that energy (ऊर्जा) which transfers from one system to another system as a result of their difference in temperature (तापमान).
  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is joule (J).
  • Generally, calorie is also used as a unit (मात्रक) (non-SI) of heat (ऊष्मा). Temperature (तापमान) :
  • Temperature (तापमान) is a thermal state of an object, which indicates the degree of hotness/coldness of a body and determines the direction of heat (ऊष्मा) flow.
  • Heat (ऊष्मा) always transfer from a substance of higher temperature (तापमान) to a substance of lower temperature. Measurement (मापन) of temperature :
  • Thermometer is an instrument used for measuring the temperature (तापमान). The melting point of wax – ≈ 60ºC The melting point of Iron – ≈ 1538ºC
  • The melting point of any solid shows the extent of the force (बल) of attraction between their particles (atoms or molecules). The high melting point of solid shows that it has a high force of attraction between particles. (ii) Boiling :
  • Process in which a liquid converts into vapour throughout the bulk of the liquid on heating is known as boiling.
  • As we all know the sunlight is a white light (प्रकाश) and it consists of seven colours. The splitting of light into its different components (VIBGYOR) i.e. violet, indigo, blue, green, yellow, orange and red is called the dispersion (विक्षेपण) of light. A prism is used to disperse light into its different components.
  • Steam at 100oC causes more severe burns than the water at the same temperature (तापमान) because steam provides more heat (ऊष्मा). The boiling point of water is 100 degree celsius. Heat (ऊष्मा) and Thermodynamics: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • The condensation process is the change of physical condition of matter from gas to liquid and it is the reverse phenomenon of vaporisation. (iv) Freezing :
  • It is a phase transition in which a liquid turns into a solid when its temperature (तापमान) is lowered below its freezing point and this process is the reverse of melting.
  • On cooling a liquid, the temperature (तापमान) at which it turns into a solid is known as its freezing point.
  • The freezing point of water is about 0ºC.
  • For a pure substance at a given temperature (तापमान) the freezing point of the liquid and the melting point of solid is the same. For example – water freezes at 0ºC and ice melts at 0ºC i.e. both points are same. Latent Heat (ऊष्मा) :
  • During a change of state, heat (ऊष्मा) is transferred without change in temperature (तापमान).
  • Latent heat (ऊष्मा) (for a process) is the amount of heat per unit (मात्रक) mass (द्रव्यमान) transferred during change of state without temperature (तापमान) change.
  • Latent heat (ऊष्मा) is mainly of two types – (i) Latent heat of fusion (melting/freezing). (ii) Latent heat of vaporisation (boiling/condensation). Latent Heat of Fusion –
  • There are lots of reasons for using alcohol than mercury as thermometric liquids. Some alcohol has a very low freezing point of about –112oC and therefore is suitable to record very low temperature (तापमान). Thus, in cold countries, alcohol is preferred to mercury as a thermometric liquid.
  • Ethylene glycol is an organic molecule (अणु) most widely used as antifreeze in automobile engines and as an industrial solvent. When it is mixed with water, the freezing point of the mixture (मिश्रण) is depressed, specifically a mixture of 60% ethylene glycol and 40% water freezes at –45 oC. That is why water is not frozen in the engine of cold countries.

Wave Motion: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • When we pluck the string of an instrument, like the Sitar, the sound (ध्वनि) that we hear is not only that of the string. The whole instrument is forced to vibrate, and it is the sound of the vibration of the instrument that we hear. When a string under tension is set into vibration, a transverse wave travels along the wire and is reflected at the fixed en...
  • The ionosphere is a region of Earth's upper atmosphere from about 50 to 400 miles altitude. It is ionized by solar radiation (विकिरण). It has practical importance because among other functions, it influences radio propagation to distant places on Earth.

Wave Motion: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Cosmic rays are not the part of electromagnetic spectrum. They are immensely high-energy (ऊर्जा) charged particles, travelling through space at a speed (चाल) approaching that of light (प्रकाश). They originated either from the sun or outside of our solar system.
  • In physics, radiation (विकिरण) is the emission or transmission of energy (ऊर्जा) in the form of waves or particles through space or through a medium. Electromagnetic radiations such as radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light (प्रकाश), X-rays and gamma rays do not require a medium. On the other hand heat (ऊष्मा) transmission by convection (संवहन) and conduction (चालन) requires a medium.
  • Albedo is the fraction of light (प्रकाश) or radiation (विकिरण) that is reflected by a body or surface. It is commonly used in astronomy to describe the reflective properties of planets, satellites, and asteroids. The range of albedo on the Earth's surface can be as high as 95% (0.95) for fresh snow cover and as little as 3% (0.03) for water.
  • The energy (ऊर्जा) released from the Sun is emitted as shortwave light (प्रकाश) and ultraviolet energy. When it reaches the Earth, some is reflected back to space (about 35%), some is absorbed by the atmosphere (about 14%), and some is absorbed in the Earth's surface (about 51%). The insolation received by the Earth is in shortwave form and heats up its surface. Th...

Wave Motion: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Shorter the wavelength (तरंगदैर्ध्य), greater is the frequency (आवृत्ति) and energy (ऊर्जा). In reference to energy, the sequence of the energy of electro- magnetic rays is as follows : Radio waves < Microwaves < Infrared < Visible light (प्रकाश) < Ultraviolet rays < X-rays < Gamma rays. It is clear that among the given options, the X-rays have the maximum amount of energy than others.
  • In the electromagnetic spectrum, gamma rays have the highest frequency (आवृत्ति) (and energy (ऊर्जा)), followed by X-rays, then ultraviolet (UV) rays, while radio waves have the lowest frequency. Thus, the correct decreasing order of their frequencies is: Gamma rays > X-rays > Ultraviolet > Radio waves, which matches option (c).
  • The correct increasing order of the given electromagnetic waves in terms of frequency (आवृत्ति) is as follows : Radio waves < Infrared rays < Visible rays < X-rays.
  • Infrared rays are commonly used in TV remote control. Remote control can be used to operate devices such as television set, DVD player or other home appliances.
  • Infrared waves are generally used in a TV remote control unit (मात्रक) to operate a TV set, but some of them use radio waves. Infrared radiation (विकिरण) and radio waves both are electromagnetic radiation.

Sound

Sound (ध्वनि): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Sound (ध्वनि) is a mechanical disturbance (pressure (दाब) wave) in a material medium that produces the sensation of hearing.
  • Sound (ध्वनि) travels in the form of waves.
  • Vibrating objects/matters produce sound (ध्वनि).
  • The substance through which sound (ध्वनि) travels is called a medium.
  • Sound (ध्वनि) waves are mechanical waves.
  • A medium (solid, liquid, gas, etc.) is necessary for their propagation; sound (ध्वनि) does not propagate in vacuum.
  • Sound (ध्वनि) is transmitted through gases, plasma (प्लाज्मा), and liquids as longitudinal waves. Through solids, however, it can be transmitted as longitudinal as well as transverse (shear) waves. Speed (चाल) of Sound :
  • Speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) depends upon the nature/properties of the medium through which it propagates.
  • Infrared rays are commonly used in TV remote control. Remote control can be used to operate devices such as television set, DVD player or other home appliances.
  • Infrared waves are generally used in a TV remote control unit (मात्रक) to operate a TV set, but some of them use radio waves. Infrared radiation (विकिरण) and radio waves both are electromagnetic radiation.

Sound (ध्वनि): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) is fastest in solids.
  • Sound (ध्वनि) does not propagate in vacuum.
  • Speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) in steel is about 17 times to speed of sound in air.
  • Speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) depends upon temperature (तापमान) in air.
  • Increase in temperature (तापमान) results to increase in the speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) (in summer season the speed of sound is more than that of winter season).
  • Humidity of air plays a very important role in the speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि).
  • Speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) in humid air is more than that of dry air. Speed of sound in different mediums S.No. Medium Sound speed Electric Current (विद्युत धारा) :
  • The rate of flow of electric charge in a conductor is termed as electric current (विद्युत धारा).
  • The speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) varies from medium to medium. Sound travels most slowly in gases, it travels faster in liquids and fastest in solids. For example – sound travels at 344 m/s in air (at 22°C), it travels at 1531 m/s in sea water and at 5950 m/s in iron (at 25°C).
  • As we know that the speed (चाल) of sound (ध्वनि) is different for different types of medium. In general, sound travels faster in liquid than gases and faster in solid than in liquid.

  • It is of two types – (i) Direct current (ii) Alternating current (i) Direct Current (DC) –
  • An electric current (विद्युत धारा) that flows only in one direction (its magnitude may be steady or varying, but direction is not reversed).
  • A battery/cell (कोशिका) is a common source of DC supply. (ii) Alternating Current (AC) –
  • An electric current (विद्युत धारा) that periodically reverses direction at regular intervals (used in mains power (शक्ति) supply).
  • Practical advantage in power (शक्ति) systems: AC voltage can be stepped up/down efficiently using transformers, enabling high-voltage transmission and low-voltage safe utilization. (Modern HVDC is also used and can be more efficient over long distances.) Electromotive Force (बल) (emf) :
  • Electromotive force (बल) (emf, ε) of a source is the work (कार्य) done per unit (मात्रक) charge by the source in driving charge around a complete circuit.
  • emf is not a force (बल); it equals the potential difference between the terminals of the source in open circuit.
  • In order for sound (ध्वनि) to propagate from one place to another, it requires a medium or a fluid to move through. The air on the Earth allows sound waves to move from one point to another. However, there is vacuum on the surface of Moon. Thus, there is no sound on the Moon.
  • Sound (ध्वनि) waves are divided into three categories that cover different frequency (आवृत्ति) range; Audible waves (20 Htz – 20,000 Htz), Infrasonic waves (< 20 Htz) and Ultrasonic waves (> 20,000 Htz). Ultrasonic waves are used to destroy insects, clean clothes by removing dust, treat diseases, control automatic doors, detection of aircraft and submarine, determin...

Sound (ध्वनि): Advanced Observations & Traps

  • Potential difference between two points is the work (कार्य) done per unit (मात्रक) charge in moving a test charge from one point to the other.
  • If work (कार्य) W is done in moving charge Q between two points, then V = W/Q
  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is volt (V), and 1 V = 1 J/C (joule per coulomb). Resistance (प्रतिरोध) (R) :
  • Resistance (प्रतिरोध) is a physical quality of conductor which resists the flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा) in the conductor. It is represented by ‘R’.
  • It is defined by R = VI.
  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is Ohm (Ω); 1Ω = 1 AV . Ohm’s Law :
  • Ohm’s Law states that for a metallic conductor (or resistor), the potential difference (voltage) across its ends is directly proportional to the current through it provided temperature (तापमान) (and other physical conditions) remain constant.
  • If V potential difference is applied at both ends of conductor and flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा) is I then, VI\  V = IR Where R is a constant, known as the resistance (प्रतिरोध) of conductor. Factors Affecting the Resistance : (i) The resistance of conductor is directly proportional to its length. or R L ----------------- (i) (ii) The resistance of conductor is inversely proportional to its cross-sectional area (A). or ----------------- (ii) Combining equations (i) & (ii) R AL\ R = ρ AL Where ρ is a proportional constant which is known as specific resistance or electrical resistivity of the material.

Electric Current (विद्युत धारा): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • The rate of flow of electric charge in a conductor is termed as electric current (विद्युत धारा).
  • The direction of the flow of positive charge is conventionally considered the direction of flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा).
  • Thus, the direction of flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा) is just opposite to that the direction of flow of electrons.
  • If a net charge Q flows through a cross-section in time t, then current I = tQ .
  • The SI unit (मात्रक) of electric current (विद्युत धारा) is ampere (A). 1 Ampere = 1 coulomb/second (1A = 1 C/s). Types of Electric Current :
  • It is of two types – (i) Direct current (ii) Alternating current (i) Direct Current (DC) –
  • An electric current (विद्युत धारा) that flows only in one direction (its magnitude may be steady or varying, but direction is not reversed).
  • A battery/cell (कोशिका) is a common source of DC supply. (ii) Alternating Current (AC) –
  • Consumption of electricity by one bulb = watt × hours1000 = 200 × 24 1000 = 4.8 units Hence, total consumption of electricity by 15 bulbs = 15 × 4.8 = 72 units
  • Consumption of electricity by one bulb = 2 units Thus, consumption of electricity by 5 bulbs = 2 × 5 = 10 units

Electric Current (विद्युत धारा): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is volt (V), and 1 V = 1 J/C (joule per coulomb). Resistance (प्रतिरोध) (R) :
  • Resistance (प्रतिरोध) is a physical quality of conductor which resists the flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा) in the conductor. It is represented by ‘R’.
  • It is defined by R = VI.
  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is Ohm (Ω); 1Ω = 1 AV . Ohm’s Law :
  • Ohm’s Law states that for a metallic conductor (or resistor), the potential difference (voltage) across its ends is directly proportional to the current through it provided temperature (तापमान) (and other physical conditions) remain constant.
  • If V potential difference is applied at both ends of conductor and flow of electric current (विद्युत धारा) is I then, VI\  V = IR Where R is a constant, known as the resistance (प्रतिरोध) of conductor. Factors Affecting the Resistance : (i) The resistance of conductor is directly proportional to its length. or R L ----------------- (i) (ii) The resistance of conductor is inversely proportional to its cross-sectional area (A). or ----------------- (ii) Combining equations (i) & (ii) R AL\ R = ρ AL Where ρ is a proportional constant which is known as specific resistance or electrical resistivity of the material.
  • Resistivity ρ depends on the nature of material and varies with temperature (तापमान) (and, in general treatment, may vary with pressure (दाब)).
  • Its SI unit (मात्रक) is Ohm-meter (Ω m). Electric Power (शक्ति) :
  • A dynamo is an electrical generator that produces direct current with the use of a commutator. It converts mechanical energy (ऊर्जा) into electrical energy.
  • Electric motor is a device which converts electrical energy (ऊर्जा) into mechanical energy. Electric motors involve rotating coils of wire which are driven by the magnetic force (बल) exerted by a magnetic field or an electric current (विद्युत धारा).

Electric Current (विद्युत धारा): Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • No. of units (kWh) = P (kW) × t (h) = 1000W× t( h) = 1000V× I× t( in hours) Electric Generator :
  • Electric Generator is a device which converts mechanical energy (ऊर्जा) into electrical energy.
  • Its works on the principle of electromagnetic induction. Transformer :
  • It is a device based on electromagnetic induction that transfers electrical energy (ऊर्जा) between AC circuits.
  • It increases (step-up) or decreases (step-down) the voltage of alternating current.
  • Transformers are of two types : (i) Step-up Transformer– It converts low voltage to high voltage of alternating current (with corresponding reduction (अपचयन) in current ideally). (ii) Step-down Transformer– It converts the high voltage into the low voltage of alternating current.
  • Transformers are used to supply appliances/devices at voltages different from the mains voltage and for efficient power (शक्ति) transmission and distribution. Electric Motor :
  • An electric motor is a device that converts electrical energy (ऊर्जा) into mechanical energy, usually by employing electromagnetic effect.
  • According to Ohm’s law, if there is a potential difference (V) across a resistor then there is a current (I) flowing through it. Current flows in a circuit as a result of difference in potential between two points in the circuit.
  • Fuse is a piece of wire with high resistance (प्रतिरोध) and very low melting point. When a high current flows through the circuit, it gets heated and melts.

Electric Current (विद्युत धारा): Advanced Observations & Traps

  • In general physics, a free electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) is an electron that can move under the influence of an applied electric (and/or magnetic) field rather than remaining bound to an atom (परमाणु) or molecule (अणु).
  • Metals with a high availability of free electrons/mobile charge carriers (and low scattering (प्रकीर्णन)) show high electrical conductivity (चालकता).
  • Silver has the highest electrical conductivity (चालकता) among common metals at room temperature (तापमान); copper, gold, and aluminium are also good conductors.
  • Electrical conduction (चालन) is also found in some liquids (e.g., electrolytes) and in gases when the gas becomes ionized (plasma (प्लाज्मा)/discharge).
  • In metals, conduction (चालन) is mainly due to the motion of electrons. In electrolytic liquids, conduction is mainly due to the motion of positive and negative ions. In ionized gases, conduction occurs due to electrons and ions.
  • Gases are generally poor conductors under ordinary conditions, but they conduct when sufficiently ionized. Gas breakdown/discharge depends on the electric field and on conditions such as the pressure (दाब)-gap product (Paschen-type behaviour); electrical discharges/arcs can occur even at atmospheric pressure (वायुमंडलीय दाब) (e.g., lightning, arc lamps/welding). Non-conductor :
  • Materials with very high electrical resistivity (very few free electrons/mobile charge carriers under normal conditions) are called insulators (non-conductors).
  • In insulators, electrons (and ions) are usually tightly bound and do not move easily, so current is extremely small under normal conditions.
  • The transformer is used to step-up and step-down the alternating current. There are two types of coils in transformers (i) Primary coil (2) Secondary coil. Alternating current flows through the primary coil which inflicts the potential in the secondary coil.
  • A mobile phone charger works on the simple principle of conversion of AC (alternating current) to DC (direct current). As we connect charger to 220 volt AC switchboard, the first job of the charger is to step down the high 220 v in 9 v or 10v. A step down transformer is used in mobile phone charger.

Conductivity (चालकता): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Electric Conductivity (चालकता) :
  • Within the atom (परमाणु), electrons nearest to the nucleus are strongly bound to the positively charged nucleus (due to protons) by electrostatic attraction.
  • Electrons farther from the nucleus (outer/valence electrons) are comparatively less tightly bound.
  • Because they are less tightly bound, such outer electrons can be removed more easily (with sufficient external energy (ऊर्जा)).
  • In metals, some outer electrons become delocalized in the solid and act as ‘conduction (चालन) (free) electrons’.
  • Free (conduction (चालन)) electrons are electrons not bound to a particular atom (परमाणु) and are free to move through the material when an external electric field is applied.
  • In general physics, a free electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) is an electron that can move under the influence of an applied electric (and/or magnetic) field rather than remaining bound to an atom (परमाणु) or molecule (अणु).
  • Metals with a high availability of free electrons/mobile charge carriers (and low scattering (प्रकीर्णन)) show high electrical conductivity (चालकता).
  • Electrical conductivity (चालकता) is a measure of the amount of electric current (विद्युत धारा) which a material can carry. The most electrically conductive element (तत्व) is silver followed by copper, aluminium and iron.
  • Copper rods are preferred for making lightning conductor because : (i) Copper is a better conductor of electricity than iron. (ii) Copper is not easily oxidized in the presence of oxygen and water molecules while iron gets rusted. (iii) Loss of energy (ऊर्जा) is much less with a copper rod than with iron rod, as copper is a bad conductor of heat (ऊष्मा) than iron.

Conductivity (चालकता): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Materials with very high electrical resistivity (very few free electrons/mobile charge carriers under normal conditions) are called insulators (non-conductors).
  • In insulators, electrons (and ions) are usually tightly bound and do not move easily, so current is extremely small under normal conditions.
  • Under sufficiently strong electric fields, an insulator (विद्युतरोधी) may conduct due to dielectric breakdown. Semiconductor (अर्धचालक) :
  • A semiconductor (अर्धचालक) is a material whose electrical conductivity (चालकता) lies between that of conductors and insulators.
  • Its conductivity (चालकता) can increase due to temperature (तापमान) (thermal generation of charge carriers) and due to controlled impurity addition (doping).
  • Adding controlled impurities to a semiconductor (अर्धचालक) is called doping; doped semiconductors are called extrinsic semiconductors.
  • Semiconductors typically show decreasing resistance (प्रतिरोध) with increasing temperature (तापमान), opposite to most metals.
  • Examples: Silicon, Germanium, Selenium, Tellurium (and α-Tin as a special elemental case). Superconductivity (अतिचालकता) :
  • If we could achieve superconductivity (अतिचालकता) at room temperature (तापमान) then we can save billions of rupee through electric transmission without any power (शक्ति) loss.
  • General Science General Studies G–142 When superconductivity (अतिचालकता) was discovered in 1911, it was found only at temperatures close to absolute zero (– 273.15°C). But since then, researchers have steadily uncovered materials that superconduct at higher temperatures. In recent years, scientists have accelerated that progress by focusing on hydrogen-rich m...

Conductivity (चालकता): Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • High-pressure (दाब) hydrogen-rich materials have shown very high Tc values. In 2019 it was discovered that lanthanum decahydride (LaH10) shows superconductivity (अतिचालकता) at around 250-260 K under megabar pressures of around 168-180 gigapascals (GPa).
  • A 2020 claim of near room-temperature (तापमान) superconductivity (अतिचालकता) in “carbonaceous sulfur hydride” was later retracted (Nature, 26 Sep 2022), and a 2023 near-ambient claim in N-doped lutetium hydride was also retracted (Nature, 7 Nov 2023). Therefore, as of March, 2026, no room temperature superconductor is confirmed at ambient conditions, and the best-confirmed very-high T c results remain in high-pressure (दाब) hydrides such as LaH10. Applications of Superconductivity : a. Powerful superconducting electromagnets (used in certain maglev designs and in research magnets. b. Magnetic resonance (अनुनाद) imaging (MRI). c. Nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) machines. d. Magnetic confinement fusion reactors (e.g. tokamaks). e. Beam steering and focusing magnets used in particle accelerators. f. Low loss power (शक्ति) cables (where implemented). Maglev :
  • Maglev is short for magnetic levitation, in which vehicles (e.g.,Trains) are supported and guided by magnetic forces.
  • Depending on the system, levitation can use magnetic attraction (EMS) or magnetic repulsion (EDS). Nuclear Fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन) :
  • In general neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) induced nuclear reactions, when bombardment of neutrons are done on an element (तत्व)'s atom (परमाणु), lighter particles such as α-particles, β-particles, protons etc. are emitted due to artificial disintegration of the atom's nucleus.
  • Nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन) is the splitting of a heavy nucleus into two lighter nuclei (fission fragments) of comparable masses, usually accompanied by neutrons and gamma radiation (विकिरण).
  • Sometimes neutrons are absorbed by a nucleus to form a heavier isotope; this is neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) capture, not nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन).
  • In fission, most energy (ऊर्जा) is released as kinetic energy (heat (ऊष्मा)) of the fission fragments; the remainder is carried by neutrons and gamma rays.
  • Germanium and silicon are the main elements which are used as a semiconductor (अर्धचालक) in transistors. Their conductivity (चालकता) on the normal/room temperature (तापमान) lies between the conductivity of conductors and insulators.
  • In solid state electronics, either pure silicon or germanium may be used as the intrinsic semiconductor (अर्धचालक) which forms the starting point for fabrication. Each has four valence electrons but germanium at a given temperature (तापमान) has more free electrons and higher conductivity (चालकता). Nuclear Physics: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • In general neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) induced nuclear reactions, when bombardment of neutrons are done on an element (तत्व)'s atom (परमाणु), lighter particles such as α-particles, β-particles, protons etc. are emitted due to artificial disintegration of the atom's nucleus.
  • Nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन) is the splitting of a heavy nucleus into two lighter nuclei (fission fragments) of comparable masses, usually accompanied by neutrons and gamma radiation (विकिरण).
  • Sometimes neutrons are absorbed by a nucleus to form a heavier isotope; this is neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) capture, not nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन).
  • In fission, most energy (ऊर्जा) is released as kinetic energy (heat (ऊष्मा)) of the fission fragments; the remainder is carried by neutrons and gamma rays.
  • Hence, nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन) is a process in which a heavy nucleus after accepting a neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) breaks down in two lighter nucleus of comparable masses.
  • In this process – a. some very fast-moving neutrons; and b. a huge amount of energy (ऊर्जा) mainly as kinetic energy (heat (ऊष्मा)) of fragments and neutrons, along with gamma rays are released.
  • The atomic bomb and nuclear reactor's principle depends upon the process of nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन).
  • The main fact relating to nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन) is that huge amount of energy (ऊर्जा) is released. This energy is known as 'Nuclear Energy'. General Science General Studies G–144
  • Germanium and silicon are the main elements which are used as a semiconductor (अर्धचालक) in transistors. Their conductivity (चालकता) on the normal/room temperature (तापमान) lies between the conductivity of conductors and insulators.
  • In solid state electronics, either pure silicon or germanium may be used as the intrinsic semiconductor (अर्धचालक) which forms the starting point for fabrication. Each has four valence electrons but germanium at a given temperature (तापमान) has more free electrons and higher conductivity (चालकता). Silicon is by far the more widely used semiconductor for electronics because it ...

Nuclear Physics: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • The temperature (तापमान) range of 107 0C and pressure (दाब) equivalent to lakhs of atmospheric pressure (वायुमंडलीय दाब) is produced on the explosion of the atom (परमाणु) bomb. Nuclear Reactor or Atomic Pile :
  • A plant in which self-sustained controlled chain nuclear reaction is maintained and at a fixed rate, energy (ऊर्जा) is obtained.
  • Such energy (ऊर्जा) is utilized for several purposes such as to produce electricity and for research works.
  • A modern reactor has the following main parts – (i) Fuel :
  • It is the main part of the reactor.
  • The matter which undergoes for fission reaction.
  • For this purpose 92U235 or 94Pu239 is used. (ii) Moderator :
  • It reduces the speed (चाल) of fast neutrons, thereby turning them into thermal neutrons capable of sustaining a nuclear chain reaction.
  • Nuclear reactor, formerly known as atomic pile (molecular furnace) uses Uranium - 235 or Plutonium-239 as fuel. When this fissile atomic nucleus absorbs a neutron (न्यूट्रॉन), it may undergo nuclear fission (नाभिकीय विखंडन). The heavy nucleus splits into two or more lighter nuclei, releasing kinetic energy (ऊर्जा), gamma radiation (विकिरण), and free neutrons. To reduce the speed (चाल) of neutrons in...
  • Nuclear fusion (नाभिकीय संलयन) reactors work (कार्य) on the principle of fusion of two light (प्रकाश) nuclei. So, statement (i) is incorrect. Nuclear fusion reactions are also called ‘Thermonuclear reactions’ because they operate at very high temperature (तापमान). Usually, nuclear fusion reactors have Tokamak design.

Nuclear Physics: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • The loss in mass (द्रव्यमान) is converted into energy (ऊर्जा).
  • For example – when two deuterons (1H2 - heavy hydrogen nucleus) fused with each other, a triton (1H3 - nucleus of tritium) or helium isotope is formed. 1H2 + 1H2 → 1H3 + P (Proton (प्रोटॉन)) or 1H2 + 1H2 → 2He3 + n (Neutron (न्यूट्रॉन))
  • Energy (ऊर्जा) released by fusion of heavy hydrogen nuclei is much more than the energy released by the fission of equal mass (द्रव्यमान) of uranium235. Hydrogen Bomb :
  • It is a nuclear fusion (नाभिकीय संलयन) bomb.
  • It is based on the fusion of heavy hydrogen nuclei.
  • Since the fusion takes place at very high temperature (तापमान) and high pressure (दाब), an atom (परमाणु) bomb is used as ignitor along with fusion bomb.
  • In the hydrogen bomb, the process of fusion is uncontrolled reaction whose application may be proved only destructive.
  • In fact, the source of the boundless energy (ऊर्जा) of the sun is the fusion of light (प्रकाश) nuclei. Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT) :

Nuclear Physics: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • The treaty defines nuclear-weapon states as those that have built and tested a nuclear explosive device before 1 January 1967; these are the United States, Russia, the United Kingdom, France, and China. Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty :
  • The Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty (CTBT) is a multilateral treaty that bans nuclear weapons test explosions and any other nuclear explosions, for both civilian and military purposes, in all environments.
  • It was adopted by the United Nations General Assembly on 10 September 1996, but has not entered into force (बल). The ratifications of nine specific nations are still required for the Treaty to enter into force.
  • Of the 44 States included in Annex 2 (of the Treaty) required for entry into force (बल) of the CTBT, all have signed with the exceptions of the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (DPRK-North Korea), India, and Pakistan. Five of the 44 Annex 2 States have signed but not ratified the CTBT (they are China, Egypt, Iran, Israel, and the United States), while Russia signed and ratified the treaty but subsequently withdrew its ratification prior to its entry into force.
  • Computer is a devi ce that can be instructed to carry out sequences of arithmetic or logical operations automatically in computer programming.
  • Data : Data is measured, collected, reported and analyzed, whereupon it can be visualized using graphs, images or other analysis tools. Data as general concepts refer to the fact that existing information or knowledge is represented or coded in some form suitable for better usage or processing.
  • Information : Information is the meaning assigned to data (facts/ideas/knowledge) using known conventions of representation. Technically data are raw facts and figures that are processed/organized/interpreted into information such as summaries and totals.
  • Bit : A bit (short for binary digit) is the smallest unit (मात्रक) of data in a computer. A bit has a single binary value either 0 or 1.
  • In Kudankulam (Tirunelveli, Tamil Nadu) two 1,000 MWe pressurized water reactor (PWR-VVER) units based on Russian technology are being erected in phase one of the project. It is used as moderator in the reactor to slow down the neutrons produced in fission so that the chain reaction can be sustained. Computer and Information Technology: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Computer is a devi ce that can be instructed to carry out sequences of arithmetic or logical operations automatically in computer programming.
  • Data : Data is measured, collected, reported and analyzed, whereupon it can be visualized using graphs, images or other analysis tools. Data as general concepts refer to the fact that existing information or knowledge is represented or coded in some form suitable for better usage or processing.
  • Information : Information is the meaning assigned to data (facts/ideas/knowledge) using known conventions of representation. Technically data are raw facts and figures that are processed/organized/interpreted into information such as summaries and totals.
  • Bit : A bit (short for binary digit) is the smallest unit (मात्रक) of data in a computer. A bit has a single binary value either 0 or 1.
  • Byte : A group of binary digits or bits (usually eight) operated on as a unit (मात्रक). A byte is considered as a unit of memory size. Development of Computers in India :
  • HEC-2M (Hollerith Electronic Computer-2M) : Computer technology came into existence in the decade of 1940 in the decade of the world. Computer age in India began in 1956 with the installation of HEC-2M (a computer designed by A.D. Booth in England) at the Indian Statistical Institute at Calcutta (now Kolkata).
  • TIFRAC (Tata Institute of Fundamental Research Automatic Calculator) : It was the first computer developed in India at the Tata Institute of Fundamental Research in Mumbai. It was formally commissioned in 1960.
  • PARAM Series : PARAM 8000 (first in the Param Series) was India's first indigenous supercomputer which was unveiled by C-DAC (Centre for Development of Advanced Computing) in 1991. Further more powerful series of computers were developed by C-DAC. Param Shivay (installed in IIT-BHU), Param Shakti (installed in IIT- Kharagpur), Param Brahma (installed in IISER, Pune), Param Pravega (installed in IISc, Bengaluru), Param Ganga (installed in IIT-Roorkee), Param Kamrupa (installed in IIT-Guwahati), Param Himalaya (installed in IIT-Mandi), Param Utkarsh (installed at C-DAC, Bengaluru), Param Smriti (installed in NABI, Mohali), Param Yukti (installed in JNCASR), Bengaluru), Param Seva (installed in IIT-Hyderabad), Param Ananta (installed in IIT-Gandhinagar), Param Sanganak (installed in IIT-Kanpur), Param Porul (installed in NIT-Tiruchirappalli), Param Siddhi-AI (installed at C-DAC, Pune) and Param Rudra systems (installed at various institutions including IUAC Delhi, IIT-Bombay, IIT-Madras, NCRA Pune, SNBNCBS Kolkata, IIT-Patna, C-DAC Delhi etc.) are some of the latest versions of Param series of computers. Computer and Information Technology: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Launched in 2015, the mission aims to enhance India’s technological prowess in supercomputing, foster research and development (R&D), and support scientific advancements across academia, industry, and government sectors.
  • NSM was set up to provide the country with supercomputing infrastructure to meet the increasing computational demands of academia, researchers, MSMEs, and startups. It is a first of its kind attempt to boost the country’s computing power (शक्ति).
  • The Mission envisages empowering our national academic and R&D institutions spread over the country by installing supercomputers of various capacities. Access to these supercomputers is provided through the National Knowledge Network (NKN). The NKN is another program of the government which connects academic institutions and R&D labs over a high-speed (चाल) network.
  • Academic and R&D institutions as well as key user departments/ministries would participate by using these facilities and develop applications of national relevance.
  • The Mission also includes development of highly professional High-Performance Computing (HPC) aware human resource for meeting challenges of development of these applications. HRD activities in this area are steered through five training centres at Pune, Kharagpur, Chennai, Palakkad, and Goa to expand the awareness and G–163 General Studies General Science familiarization of supercomputing with college students and researchers.
  • Under NSM, as of 30 November, 2025, a total of 37 supercomputers with cumulative compute capacity of 39 Petaflops, have been deployed across various academic institutions, research organizations, and R&D labs, including prominent institutions like IISc, IITs, C-DAC, and other institutions from Tier-II and Tier III cities of the country under NSM.
  • The supercomputing systems commissioned under NSM have achieved an overall utilization rate of over 85%, with many systems exceeding 95%, demonstrating a high level of usage and efficiency in their computational capacity.
  • NSM is steered jointly by Department of Science and Technology (DST) and Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) and implemented by Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC), Pune and Indian Institute of Science (IISc), Bengaluru. Classification of Computer :

  • Supercomputers can combine thousands to millions of processors/cores and they complete suitable compute- intensive workloads by parallel processing.
  • Supercomputers are very expensive in cost so smaller companies can not afford it.
  • These are used primarily for scientific and engineering work (कार्य) requiring extremely high-speed (चाल) computation.
  • Applications of supercomputers commonly include weather/climate modeling, physical simulations, computational chemistry/material science, and complex engineering computations.
  • Supercomputers are used in the following areas – (i) Weather forecasting (ii) Oil & Gas exploration (iii) Physical simulations (iv) Quantum mechanics (v) Climate research (vi) Molecular modelling

However, this supercomputer is not developed/manufactured in India. It is purchased from its manufacturer DELL, USA.

  • Details of such generations are as follows – Generation Hardware Software Characteristics Example I 1940–1956 Vacuum tubes; memory technology varied (e.g., mercury delay lines/electro- static storage/ magnetic drum in some systems) Machine language Very large size; very expensive; high power (शक्ति) consumption; limited applications ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator and Computer), EDSAC (Electronic Delay Storage Automatic Calculator), UNIVAC (Universal Automatic Computer) II 1956–1963 Transistors; magnetic- core memory; magnetic tape & magnetic disk (secondary storage) FORTRAN, COBOL Smaller; faster; more reliable; higher storage capacity than Gen I IBM 1620, IBM 7094, UNIV AC 1107 III 1964–1971 Integrated circuits (ICs) Operating systems; high-level languages (e.g., BASIC) Smaller; faster; more reliable; broader commercial/scientific applications Minicomputers (e.g., early IC-based minicomputer systems) IV 1971–Present (microproce- ssor era) Microprocessor- based systems (LSI/VLSI); semiconductor (अर्धचालक) memory Spreadsheet software; database software; graphical user interface (GUI) Fast; comparatively cheap; widely used in homes/business Microcomputers (personal computers) V Present and beyond (AI-focused/ advanced computing) Parallel processing; advanced semi- conductor integration; multimedia-capable I/O; optical storage; networking/Internet technologies Internet and appli- cations; multimedia applications; AI software; modern languages such as C, C++, Java, and C#/ VB.NET/F# (on the .NET platform) Very small; very fast; easy to use; multifaceted applications (sound (ध्वनि), visual & text) Notebook/Laptop; Palmtop/handheld class devices *Note : There have been supercomputers in all generations of computers. Supercomputers are typically defined as a class of extremely powerful high-performance computers used mainly for scientific/engineering computations. So, the very fastest computers of any generation are supercomputers. However, the term 'supercomputer' was coined around the mid-1960s (e.g., CDC 6600 era). Main parts of Computer :

  • Non-volatile computer memory retains data even when power (शक्ति) is removed, examples include ROM (Read-Only Memory), flash memory (like in SSDs and USB drives), and hard disk drives.
  • RAM is an acronym for random-access memory. It is considered as temporary memory and works only when the computer is functioning. Once the computer is switched off, all the data which is stored in RAM are lost or erased.

Defence Technology: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • The Integrated Guided Missile Development Programme (IGMDP) was conceived by renowned scientist Dr. APJ Abdul Kalam (then director of Defence Research and Development Laboratory – DRDL) to enable India to attain self-sufficiency in the field of missile technology.
  • The IGMDP finally got the approval from the Govt. of India on 26 July, 1983. This ambitious project brought together the country’s scientific community, academic institutions, R & D laboratories and industries in giving shapes to the strategic, indigenous missile system.
  • IGMDP was started in 1983 and completed in March, 2012.
  • The missiles developed under this programme are : a. Short-range surface to surface ballistic missile – Prithvi b. Intermediate-range surface to surface ballistic missile – Agni (As re-entry 'Technology Demonstrator' project; technology used for Agni series of missiles) c. Short-range low-level surface to air missile – Trishul (Technology Demonstrator – not inducted) d. Medium-range surface to air missile – Akash e. Third Generation anti-tank missile – Nag
  • Dr. Kalam played a major role in the development and operationlisation of Agni and Prithvi Missiles. Prithvi Missile :

  • Its first test was conducted on 25 February, 1988 from (SHAR)- Sriharikota High Altitude Range.
  • Its variants are as follows – (a) Prithvi - I (ss-150) – Army version : 150 km range with a warhead of 1000 kg. (b) Prithvi - II (ss-250) – Air Force (बल) version : 250 km range with a warhead of 500-750 kg. (c) Prithvi - III (ss-350)/Dhanush – Naval version : 350 km range with a warhead of 1000 kg.

Defence Technology: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • MTCR was formed in 1987 by the G-7 industrialized countries (Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan, the UK, and the United States).
  • There are currently 35 countries that are members (Partners) of the MTCR : Argentina (1993); Australia (1990); Austria (1991); Belgium (1990); Brazil (1995); Bulgaria (2004); Canada (1987); Czech Republic (1998); Denmark (1990); Finland (1991); France (1987); Germany (1987); Greece (1992); Hungary (1993); Iceland (1993); India (2016); Ireland (1992); Italy (1987); Japan (1987); Luxembourg (1990); Netherlands (1990); New Zealand (1991); Norway (1990); Poland (1998); Portugal (1992); South Korea (Republic of Korea) (2001); Russian Federation (1995); South Africa (1995); Spain (1990); Sweden (1991); Switzerland (1992); Turkiye (Turkey) (1997); Ukraine (1998); United Kingdom (1987); United States of America (1987). The date in brackets represents the initial year of membership.
  • The MTCR seeks to limit the risks of proliferation of weapons of mass (द्रव्यमान) destruction (WMD) by controlling exports of goods and technologies that could make a contribution to delivery systems (other than manned aircraft) for such weapons. In this context, the Regime places particular focus on rockets and unmanned aerial vehicles capable of delivering a payload of at least 500 kg to a range of at least 300 km and on equipment, software, and technology for such systems.
  • The MTCR is not a treaty and does not impose any legally binding obligations on Partners (members). Rather, it is an informal political understanding among states that seek to limit the proliferation of missiles and missile technology. The Hague Code of Conduct against Ballistic Missile Pro- liferation (HCoC) :
  • The Hague Code of Conduct against Ballistic Missile Proliferation (HCoC) is the result of efforts of the international community to internationally regulate the area of ballistic missiles capable of carrying weapons of mass (द्रव्यमान) destruction (WMD).
  • The Hague Code of Conduct (HCoC), was established on 25 November, 2002 as an arrangement to prevent the proliferation of ballistic missiles.
  • The HCoC is the only multilateral code in the area of disarmament which has been adopted over the last years. The HCoC does not ban ballistic missiles, but calls for maximum possible restraint in the development, testing and deployment of ballistic missiles capable of delivering WMD.
  • Along with the MTCR, the HCoC is one of the only multilateral instruments addressing ballistic missile proliferation; the HCOC is the transparency and confidence building measure concerning the spread of ballistic missiles.
  • A supersonic cruise missile 'BrahMos' is a joint venture between the Russian Federation's NPO Mashinostroyenia and DRDO. The name BrahMos is formed from the names of two rivers, the Brahmaputra of India and the Moskva of Russia. It has a speed (चाल) of 2.8 to 3.0 Mach, with a original range of 290 Km. The submarine variant of BrahMos was test-fired succe...

The missile successfully hit the target with pin-point accuracy after performing high-level and extremely complex manoeuvres. BrahMos a...

Scientists & Inventions: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Albert Einstein (1879-1955) :
  • German-born physicist who developed the special and general theories of relativity and won the Nobel Prize for Physics in 1921 for his services to Theoretical Physics, and especially for his discovery of the law of the photoelectric effect.
  • Einstein is generally considered the most influential physicist of the 20th Century. Einstein miracle year 1905 –
  • While working at the patent office, he did some of the most creative works of his life.
  • First Paper – He applied the quantum theory (Developed by German Physicist Max Planck) to light (प्रकाश) in order to explain the phenomenon known as the photoelectric effect, by which a material will emit electrically charged particles when hit by light.
  • Second Paper – Einstein's second paper of 1905, titled "On the Motion of Small Particles Suspended in a Stationary Liquid, as Required by the Molecular-Kinetic Theory of Heat (ऊष्मा)", provided a theoretical, mathematical explanation for Brownian motion. it provided crucial evidence for the existence of atoms and molecules, helping to convince skeptical physicists of their reality.
  • Third Paper – In the third and most famous article titled “On the Electrodynamics of Moving Bodies” Einstein confronted the apparent contradiction between two principal theories of physics : Isaac Newton concepts of absolute space and time and James Clerk Maxwell’s idea (via electromagnetism) that the speed (चाल) of light (प्रकाश) is a constant. To do this he introduced his Special Theory of Relativity which held that the laws of physics are the same even for objects moving in different inertial frames (i.e. at constant speeds relative to each other) and that the speed of light is constant in all inertial frames.
  • Fourth paper concerned the fundamental relationship between mass (द्रव्यमान) and energy (ऊर्जा). Einstein famous equation E = mc2 (where ‘c’ is the constant speed (चाल) of light (प्रकाश)) expressed this relationship.
  • ‘Khanjar-XII’ was a bilateral joint special forces exercise held from 10-23 March, 2025 in Kyrgyzstan, between India and Kyrgyzstan. Russia, Turkmenistan, and Mongolia did not participate in it. The 13 th (XIII) edition of the India- Kyrgyzstan joint special forces exercise 'Khanjar' was held from 4-17 February, 2026 in Missamari, Assam.
  • In 1718 an Englishman named James Puckle (1667-1724) invented defence gun what proved to be one of the keys steps in the evolution (जैव विकास) of mechanical machine guns. That year Puckle, a London solicitor patented what he called the Defence Gun. There is some argument among historians about whether this was the first patent of a manually operated revolving-...

Scientists & Inventions: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • His earliest researches in optics and acoustics-the two fields of investigation to which he has dedicated his entire career were carried out while he was a student.
  • Since at that time a scientific career did not appear to present the best possibilities, Raman joined the Indian Finance Department in 1907, though the duties of his office took most of his time, Raman found opportunities for carrying on experimental research in the laboratory of the Indian Association for the Cultivation of Science at Calcutta (now Kolkata) of which he became Honorary Secretary in 1919.
  • In 1917 he was offered the newly endowed Palit Chair of Physics at Calcutta University and decided to accept it. After 15 years at Calcutta he became Professor at the G–296 General Studies General Science Indian Institute of Science at Bangalore.
  • He was elected a Fellow of the Royal Society early in his career (1924) and was Knighted in 1929. In 1930 he was awarded Nobel Prize for his work (कार्य) Raman-Effect. Satyendra Nath Bose (1894-1974) :
  • Satyendra Nath Bose [Born Jan. 1, 1894, Calcutta (now Kolkata) – died Feb. 4, 1974], was great Indian Mathematician and Physicist noted for his collaboration with Albert Einstein in developing a theory regarding the gaslike qualities of Electromagnetic radiation (विकिरण) (Bose- Einstein Statistics).
  • Bose, a graduate of the University of Calcutta taught at the University of Dhaka (1921-1945) and then at Calcutta (1945-1956). Bose’s numerous scientific papers published contributed to statistical mechanics and thermoluminescence and unified field theory. Bose’s Planck’s Law and the Hypothesis of Light (प्रकाश) Quanta (1924) led Einstein to seek him out for a collaboration.
  • A Fellow of the Royal Society, he was awarded India’s second-highest civilian award, the Padma Vibhushan, in 1954 by the Govt. of India.
  • The class of particles that obey Bose-Einstein statistics– bosons– was named after Bose by Paul Dirac.
