Scientists & Inventions: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • ISCA has been promoting science and inculcating the spirit of science through its multifarious activities.
  • The ISCA is a society originally registered under Societies Act XXI of 1860; now deemed registered under the West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961.
  • The ISCA vision is to inculcate scientific temper among the common people.
  • The ISCA mission is to make people aware about the recent developments in science and technology and its impact on the society, to establish different centres all over India for spreading scientific knowledge through school / college programs, to provide a common platform where scientists from India and abroad can exchange their views.
  • The first Indian Science Congress was held in 1914 at the premises of the Asiatic Society, Calcutta (Now Kolkata).
  • The 104th ISC conference was held at Sri Venkateshwar University, Tirupati, (A.P.) from 3-7 Jan 2017; 105 th conference held at Manipur University (Imphal) from 16-20 March 2018; 106th conference was held at Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, Jalandhar, Punjab from 3-7 Jan. 2019 and its focal theme was– ‘Future India : Science &Technology’.
  • Its 107th conference was held at University of Agricultural Sciences, GKVK Campus, Bengaluru with the focal theme of ‘Science & Technology : Rural Development' from 3-7 Jan. 2020.
  • Due to COVID-19 pandemic annual ISC conference was cancelled in 2021 and 2022.
  • Scientist Albert Einstein is famous for giving the simple and factual explanation of Photoelectric effect on the basis of Planck's quantum theory. He was awarded Nobel Prize in Physics in 1921 for his services to Theoritical Physics, and especially for his discovery of the law of the photoelectric effect.
  • In 1930, C.V. Raman was the first non-white, Asian and Indian to receive the Nobel Prize in Physics for the work (कार्य) on scattering (प्रकीर्णन) of light (प्रकाश) and discovery of Raman Effect. Every year 28 th February is celebrated as National Science Day. It is because on the same day in 1928 he discovered 'Raman Effect'.

Scientists & Inventions: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • The branch of technology that deals with the dimensions and tolerances of less than 100 nanometres, especially the manipulation of individual atoms and molecules.
  • Nanometre is a unit (मात्रक) of length (1 nanometre = 10–9 metre)
  • The matter on nanoscale is too small which cannot be seen by the naked eyes, and even with conventional lab/ optical microscopes; nanoscale is studied using advanced tools such as electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) microscopes and scanning probe microscopes.
  • To observe matter on nanoscale Scanning Tunneling Microscope or Atomic Force (बल) Microscope is used.
  • Eminent physicist Richard Feynman firstly expressed his views on this subject at California Institute of Technology (Caltech) on 29 Dec. 1959 and initiated an era of nanotechnology.
  • The term nanotechnology was firstly defined by Tokyo Science University Professor Norio Taniguchi in 1974. According to him nanotechnology mainly consists of the processing, separation, consolidation and deformation of materials by one atom (परमाणु) or by one molecule (अणु).
  • In 1986, Dr. K. Eric Drexler wrote a book on nano- technology – 'Engines of Creation: The coming era of Nanotechnology', which is the first book on this subject. LASER :
  • A laser is a device that emits light (प्रकाश) through a process of optical amplification based on the stimulated emission of electromagnetic radiation (विकिरण).
  • J.V . Narlikar propounded the ‘New Theory of Relativity’. In collaboration with a senior scientist Fred Hoyle, he propounded this theory by amending the Einstein’s Theory of Relativity.
  • According to Indian astrophysicist Jayant Vishnu (J.V .) Narlikar, astrology is not a science.

Physics : Miscellaneous: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Indian Science Congress Association (ISCA) :
  • The Indian Science Congress Association is an autonomous scientific society/professional body in the DST (Department of Science & Technology, Government of India) family; supported through grant-in-aid.
  • The ISCA is a premier scientific organization of the country established in 1914, with headquarters at Kolkata (W.B.). It owes its origin to the foresight and initiative of two British scientists, namely, Prof. J.L. Simonsen (chemist) and Prof. P.S. MacMahon (mathematician).
  • ISCA has been promoting science and inculcating the spirit of science through its multifarious activities.
  • The ISCA is a society originally registered under Societies Act XXI of 1860; now deemed registered under the West Bengal Societies Registration Act, 1961.
  • The ISCA vision is to inculcate scientific temper among the common people.
  • The ISCA mission is to make people aware about the recent developments in science and technology and its impact on the society, to establish different centres all over India for spreading scientific knowledge through school / college programs, to provide a common platform where scientists from India and abroad can exchange their views.
  • The first Indian Science Congress was held in 1914 at the premises of the Asiatic Society, Calcutta (Now Kolkata).
  • National Physical Laboratory is situated in New Delhi.
  • The National Chemical Laboratory (CSIR-NCL) Pune was established in 1950 is a constituent laboratory of the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR). CSIR-NCL is a science and knowledge-based research, development and consulting organization.

Physics : Miscellaneous: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Nanometre is a unit (मात्रक) of length (1 nanometre = 10–9 metre)
  • The matter on nanoscale is too small which cannot be seen by the naked eyes, and even with conventional lab/ optical microscopes; nanoscale is studied using advanced tools such as electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) microscopes and scanning probe microscopes.
  • To observe matter on nanoscale Scanning Tunneling Microscope or Atomic Force (बल) Microscope is used.
  • Eminent physicist Richard Feynman firstly expressed his views on this subject at California Institute of Technology (Caltech) on 29 Dec. 1959 and initiated an era of nanotechnology.
  • The term nanotechnology was firstly defined by Tokyo Science University Professor Norio Taniguchi in 1974. According to him nanotechnology mainly consists of the processing, separation, consolidation and deformation of materials by one atom (परमाणु) or by one molecule (अणु).
  • In 1986, Dr. K. Eric Drexler wrote a book on nano- technology – 'Engines of Creation: The coming era of Nanotechnology', which is the first book on this subject. LASER :
  • A laser is a device that emits light (प्रकाश) through a process of optical amplification based on the stimulated emission of electromagnetic radiation (विकिरण).
  • The term LASER originated as an acronym for “Light (प्रकाश) Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation (विकिरण)”.
  • The Nano Hummingbird or Nano Air Vehicle (NA V) is a tiny, remote controlled aircraft built to resemble and fly like a hummingbird, developed in the United States by AeroVironment Inc.
  • Indian-American engineer and entrepreneur Vinod Dham is known in Silicon Valley as the ‘Father of the Pentium Chip,’ because he led the development of Intel’s famous Pentium microprocessor. The Government of India awarded him the Padma Bhushan 2025 (Science and Engineering) for this historic contribution.

Physics : Miscellaneous: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Masers are used as the timekeeping device in atomic clocks, and as extremely low-noise microwave amplifiers in radio telescopes and deep space spacecraft communication ground stations.
  • Modern masers can be designed to generate electromagnetic waves at not only microwave frequencies but also radio and infrared frequencies. For this reason Charles Townes suggested replacing "microwave" with the word "molecular" as the first word in the acronym maser.
  • The laser works by the same principle as the maser, but produces higher frequency (आवृत्ति) coherent radiation (विकिरण) at optical wavelengths. Liquid Crystal :
  • A liquid crystal is a thermodynamically stable phase characterized by anisotropy of properties without the existence of a three-dimensional crystal lattice, generally lying in the temperature (तापमान) range between the solid and isotropic liquid phase, hence the term mesophase.
  • Liquid crystal materials are unique in their properties and uses. As research into this field continues and as new applications are developed, liquid crystals will play an important role in modern technology.
  • Liquid crystal materials generally have several common characteristics. Among these are a rod like molecular structure, rigidity of the long axis, and strong dipole and/ or easily polarizable substituents.
  • Liquid crystals find wide use in liquid crystal displays, which rely on the optical properties of certain liquid crystalline substances in the presence or absence of an electric field.
  • Liquid crystals have a multitude of other uses. They are used for nondestructive mechanical testing of materials under stress. This technique is also used for the visualization of RF (radio frequency (आवृत्ति)) waves in waveguides. They are used in medical applications where, for example, transient pressure (दाब) transmitted by a walking foot on the ground is measured. Low molar mass (द्रव्यमान) (LMM) liquid crystals have applications including erasable optical disks, full color "electronic slides" for computer-aided drawing (CAD), and light (प्रकाश) modulators for color electronic imaging. Indian Antarctic Programme :
  • 'Kalpana Chawala' was an astronaut and space shuttle mission specialist of STS-107 (Columbia), who was killed when the craft disintegrated after re-entry into Earth's atmosphere. "I am the citizen of milky way", this statement has been credited to Kalpana Chawala.
  • The Department of Atomic Energy (ऊर्जा) (DAE) of Indian government commissioned the first heavy water plant at Nangal, Punjab in the premises of National Fertilisers Limited in 1962.

Physics : Miscellaneous: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • India launched its first scientific expedition to the Arctic Ocean in 2007 and opened a research base (क्षार) named "Himadri" at the International Arctic Research Base at Ny-Alesund, Svalbard, Norway in July, 2008 for carrying out studies in disciplines like Glaciology, Atmospheric sciences & Biological sciences.
  • IndARC is India's first multi-sensor underwater moored observatory in the Arctic region. It was deployed in 2014 at Kongsfjorden fjord, Svalbard, Norway which is midway between Norway and North Pole. Its research goal is to study the Arctic climate and its influence on the monsoon. Kanad (कणाद) :
  • Kanad, also known as Kashyap, Uluka and Kanabhuk, was an Indian natural scientist and Philosopher, who founded the Vaishesika School of Indian Philosophy.
  • Estimated to have lived sometimes between 6th century to 2nd century BC, little is known about his life. He is known for developing the foundation of an atomistic approach of the universe.
  • He suggested that everything can be subdivided, but this subdivision can not go on forever and there must be smallest entities (Parmanu) that cannot be divided, that are eternal, that aggregate in different ways to yield complex substances and bodies with unique identity, a process that involves heat (ऊष्मा) and this is the basis for all material existence. Dalton’s Atomic Theory :
  • In the beginning of 19th century, Dalton proposed that all matters are composed of atoms which are indivisible and indestructible building blocks.
  • All atoms of an element (तत्व) are identical.
  • Different elements have atoms of different size and mass (द्रव्यमान).
  • A microphone is acoustic to electric transducer or sensor that converts sound (ध्वनि) into an electrical signal. The carbon microphone was used in early telephone repeaters, making long distance phone calls possible in the era before vacuum tube amplifiers. Carbon microphones can be used as amplifiers.
  • The three laws of planetary motions were proposed by Johannes Kepler in early 1600s. Kepler made it clear that every planet revolves around the Sun in an elliptical orbit.

Atomic Structure: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Kanad, also known as Kashyap, Uluka and Kanabhuk, was an Indian natural scientist and Philosopher, who founded the Vaishesika School of Indian Philosophy.
  • Estimated to have lived sometimes between 6th century to 2nd century BC, little is known about his life. He is known for developing the foundation of an atomistic approach of the universe.
  • He suggested that everything can be subdivided, but this subdivision can not go on forever and there must be smallest entities (Parmanu) that cannot be divided, that are eternal, that aggregate in different ways to yield complex substances and bodies with unique identity, a process that involves heat (ऊष्मा) and this is the basis for all material existence. Dalton’s Atomic Theory :
  • In the beginning of 19th century, Dalton proposed that all matters are composed of atoms which are indivisible and indestructible building blocks.
  • All atoms of an element (तत्व) are identical.
  • Different elements have atoms of different size and mass (द्रव्यमान).
  • Atoms are neither created nor could be destroyed. This implies that during chemical reactions, no atoms are created or destroyed.
  • Atoms of different elements combine in a fixed ratio to form compounds.
  • The constituents of an atom (परमाणु) are protons, neutrons and electrons. The protons and neutrons (nucleons) are found in the nucleus of atoms. The nucleus of an atom is surrounded by electrons.
  • (I) Discovery of pi meson – 1947 (by Cecil Powell) (II) Discovery of neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) – 1932 (by James Chadwick) (III) Discovery of electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) – 1897 (by J.J. Thomson) (IV) Discovery of proton (प्रोटॉन) – 1919 (by Rutherford) Hence, the correct chronological order is III, IV , II, I which is given in option (b). Note : Proton was first observed by Goldstein as canal/an...

Atomic Structure: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Atoms of different elements are with different atomic number.
  • The atomic number of Hydrogen is 1, it means it has one proton (प्रोटॉन) in its nucleus. Mass (द्रव्यमान) Number (A) :
  • (A) has been derived from German word Atomgewicht which means atomic weight (भार).
  • The total number of protons and neutrons in an atom (परमाणु)’s nucleus is known as its mass (द्रव्यमान) number.
  • It is used as the nucleon count and approximates the atomic mass (द्रव्यमान) of a nuclide in atomic mass units.
  • Mass (द्रव्यमान) number of an atom (परमाणु) = No. of protons + No. of neutrons in nucleus of an atom.
  • It represents the total number of nucleons in the nucleus of an atom (परमाणु).
  • The mass (द्रव्यमान) number is not a fundamental character of the element (तत्व).
  • The positron or antielectron is the antiparticle or the antimatter counterpart of the electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन). The positron has an positive electric charge and has the same mass (द्रव्यमान) as an electron.
  • In particle physics, an elementary particle or fundamental particle is a subatomic particle with no sub-structure, thus not composed of other particles. Particles currently thought to be elementary include the fundamental fermions (quarks, leptons, antiquarks and antileptons) as well as fundamental bosons.

Atomic Structure: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Different element (तत्व)’s atom (परमाणु) with the same number of neutrons are called Isoneutronic or Isotones. Example – a. 1H3 and 2He4 – Both have same number of neutrons i.e. 2 neutrons. b. 14Si30, 15P31, 16S32 – All with the same number of neutrons i.e. 16 neutrons. c. 6C13, 7N14 – both have the same number of neutrons i.e. 7 neutrons. Radioactivity (रेडियोधर्मिता) :
  • Radioactivity (रेडियोधर्मिता) is a nuclear phenomenon in which there is a spontaneous emission of radiations from the nuclei of atoms during their decay. In other words, radioactive decay is the process by which an unstable atomic nucleus loses energy (ऊर्जा) by radiation (विकिरण). Unstable nuclei that exist in nature are said to undergo natural radioactivity, and the man made unstable nuclei undergo artificial radioactivity.
  • The elements with such atoms have spontaneous disintegration character.
  • Such elements are called radioactive substances.
  • α particles, β particles and γ rays are emitted by radioactive nuclei. These three types of radiation (विकिरण) are called ionizing radiation.
  • Radioactivity (रेडियोधर्मिता) is a nuclear phenomenon, not a chemical reaction.
  • It was discovered by Henri Becquerel in Uranium.
  • In 1898 Marie Curie and her husband Pierre Curie discovered a radioactive element (तत्व) Polonium.
  • The Isotopes (समस्थानिक) are a set of nuclides/atoms having the same number of protons, but a different number of neutrons. In other words, the same atomic number (Z) but having different mass (द्रव्यमान) number (A). Each individual isotope has a separate nuclei. The Isotopes that are unstable and undergo radioactive decay are called radioisotopes.
  • Statement 1 is correct. Atoms of the same element (तत्व) having the same atomic number (number of protons) but different mass (द्रव्यमान) number (due to different number of neutrons) are called isotopes (समस्थानिक). Therefore, their chemical properties are generally similar, while some physical properties (e.g., mass, stability) may differ. Examples: Oxygen-16, Oxygen-17 and Oxy...

Physical and Chemical Changes, Solution: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Matter is defined as anything that occupies space and exhibits rest mass (द्रव्यमान) or inertia.
  • In physical changes no new substances are formed; the substance retains the same chemical composition while only its physical properties change. Physical changes may be reversible; some physical changes are irreversible, even though no new substance is formed.
  • In our daily life, we encounter many changes in our surroundings, though we do not notice them, such as souring of milk, rusting of iron, stretching of a rubber band, bread becoming toast, melting of wax, etc.
  • All these changes that take place around us are either a physical change or chemical change. Physical Changes
  • Physical changes, are the changes that change the physical traits of the substance, without making any change in their internal structure.
  • Radioactivity (रेडियोधर्मिता) was discovered in 1896 by the French Scientist Henri Becquerel when he was working with phosphorescent materials. Physical and Chemical Changes, Solution Notes • Matter is defined as anything that occupies space and exhibits rest mass (द्रव्यमान) or inertia. • In physical changes no new substances are formed; the substance retains the same chemic...
  • G–333 General Studies General Science Chemical Changes • A chemical change is defined as the process in which the atoms of one or more substances are rearranged or combine to form a new substance. • When a substance undergoes a chemical change, the chemical properties of the substance change and it is transformed into a different substance with di...
  • Change of water into the vapour is a physical change.
  • When some impurities are mixed with any liquid, its boiling point increases. On the other hand, the freezing point generally gets lowered below 0 oC when ice is mixed with salts (NaCl or KNO 3 etc). Its mixture (मिश्रण) is called freezing- mixture. That is why statement (A) is correct, while reason (R) is wrong.

Physical and Chemical Changes, Solution: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • In physical changes no chemical bonds are broken or formed.
  • Some examples of physical changes are : (i) Melting of wax (ii) Melting of ice (iii) Freezing of water (iv) Vaporization of water (v) Dissolving sugar in water (vi) Chopping of wood (vii) Crumpling of paper Classification of Elements
  • Periodic Table or Periodic table of elements is a tabular arrangement of the chemical elements, arranged by atomic number, electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) configuration and recurring chemical properties, whose structure shows periodic trends. Need for classification of Elements :
  • Before the beginning of the 18 th century, only a few elements were known, so it was quite easy to study and remember the properties of those elements and their compounds individually.
  • Later on, with the growing number of known elements, it became more and more difficult to study their properties individually, therefore the need for their systematic classification was felt. Development of Periodic Table :
  • Pasteurization is the partial sterilization of a product, such as milk, wine or fruit juices to make it safe for consumption and improve its keeping quality. Pasteurization was developed by Louis Pasteur in 1864. It is not used for tea. Other pairs are correctly matched.

It is an example of an oil in water emulsion. A colloid is a mixture (मिश्रण) in which one substance consisting of microscopically dispersed insoluble particles is suspended throughout another substance. ...
  • Among the given options, honey is not a colloid. At room temperature (तापमान), honey is a supercooled liquid, in which the glucose precipitates into solid granules. This forms a semisolid solution of precipitated glucose crystals in a solution of fructose and other ingredients. Milk, blood and icecream are colloids.

Physical and Chemical Changes, Solution: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Several major attempts were made for classification of elements. Some of them are as follows - (i) Prout Hypothesis (1815-16) –
  • William Prout observed that the atomic weights that had been measured for the elements known at that time appeared to be whole multiples of the atomic weight (भार) of hydrogen.
  • He hypothesized that the hydrogen atom (परमाणु) was the only truly fundamental object, which he called protyle and that the atoms of other elements were actually groupings of various numbers of hydrogen atoms.
  • Later, more precise measurements of atomic weights showed many values were not exact integer multiples of hydrogen, so chemists rejected this simple form of the hypothesis, even though a modified ‘whole-number rule’ for isotopes (समस्थानिक) remains approximately valid. (ii) Dobereiner’s Law of Triads (1829) –
  • According to this law, when chemically analogous elements are arranged in order of increasing atomic weight (भार), they formed well-marked group of three called ‘Triads’, in which the atomic weight of middle element (तत्व) was nearly equal to the arithmetic mean of the other.
  • If an unsaturated sugar solution is prepared by mixing 100 grams of sugar with half liter of water then volume of the solution remain constant.
  • Osmosis is the process when water (or solvent) molecules move from a region (solution) of low concentration to a region (solution) of higher concentration through a semi- permeable membrane. When salt (लवण) is applied to cucumber or cut onion pieces, it makes their surrounding more concentrated than the concentration of their cell (कोशिका) sap. Then it causes the...
  • If the semipermeable membrane is placed between the solvent and solution, the solvent molecules will flow through the membrane from pure solvent (lower concentration) to the solution (higher concentration). This process of flow of solvent is called osmosis, not reverse osmosis. Hence, only statement (1) is correct.
  • Distillation is the action of purifying a liquid by a process of heating and cooling. Distillation is generally used for purification of liquid impurities while sublimation and crystallization are used for purification of solid impurities.
  • Sublimation is the transition of a substance directly from the solid to the gas phase, without passing through the intermediate liquid phase. Substances like camphor, naphthalene or dry ice (solid carbon dioxide) display this phenomenon.

Inorganic Chemistry: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Classification of Elements
  • Periodic Table or Periodic table of elements is a tabular arrangement of the chemical elements, arranged by atomic number, electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) configuration and recurring chemical properties, whose structure shows periodic trends. Need for classification of Elements :
  • Before the beginning of the 18 th century, only a few elements were known, so it was quite easy to study and remember the properties of those elements and their compounds individually.
  • Later on, with the growing number of known elements, it became more and more difficult to study their properties individually, therefore the need for their systematic classification was felt. Development of Periodic Table :
  • Several major attempts were made for classification of elements. Some of them are as follows - (i) Prout Hypothesis (1815-16) –
  • William Prout observed that the atomic weights that had been measured for the elements known at that time appeared to be whole multiples of the atomic weight (भार) of hydrogen.
  • He hypothesized that the hydrogen atom (परमाणु) was the only truly fundamental object, which he called protyle and that the atoms of other elements were actually groupings of various numbers of hydrogen atoms.
  • Later, more precise measurements of atomic weights showed many values were not exact integer multiples of hydrogen, so chemists rejected this simple form of the hypothesis, even though a modified ‘whole-number rule’ for isotopes (समस्थानिक) remains approximately valid. (ii) Dobereiner’s Law of Triads (1829) –
  • The most abundant element (तत्व) in the Universe is Hydrogen, which makes up about 3/4 of all matter. Helium makes up most of the remaining 1/4. Thus, it is clear that hydrogen is the most common and abundant element in the Universe. While the most abundant element in the Earth's crust is Oxygen making up 46.6% of Earth's crust.

Inorganic Chemistry: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • All elements present in a group generally have similar electronic configuration in their valence shell and have the same number of valence electrons for main-group elements; transition metals are an important exception. Periods :
  • There are seven horizontal rows in the modern periodic table.
  • The elements in a period have consecutive atomic numbers.
  • The periods have been numbered from 1 to 7 (Arabic numerals).
  • In each period a new shell starts filling up. The period number is also the number of shells that starts filling up as we move from left to right across the period. (a) The first period is the shortest period of all. It contains only two elements – H and He. (b) The second and third periods are called short-periods each with 8 elements. (c) The 4th & 5th periods are called long periods each with 18 elements. (d) The 6th & 7th periods are very long periods; both presently have 32 known elements. Types of Elements :
  • The Earth's crust is the major source of metals.
  • Among non-metals – Oxygen and then Silicon and among metals – Aluminium is found in abundant quantity in the earth crust. Note : Silicon is sometimes considered as a metalloid, meaning it has properties of both metals and non- metals.
  • In nature, metals are found in two states : (i) Free (native) state (ii) Combined state
  • Chemically diamond is the purest form of Carbon (crystal structure). Hence, it is a fundamental element (तत्व). Sand is basically made up of Silicon and Oxygen, Marble is made up of Calcium, Carbon and Oxygen while sugar is mainly the compound (यौगिक) of Carbon, Hydrogen and Oxygen.

Alloys: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • An alloy (मिश्र धातु) is a mixture (मिश्रण) that may be homogeneous or heterogeneous.
  • It is a metallic substance (either a compound (यौगिक) or a solution) composed of two or more chemical elements, of which at least one is a metal.
  • An alloy (मिश्र धातु) has bulk metallic properties different from those of its constituent elements.
  • Steel is an alloy (मिश्र धातु) of iron and carbon containing less than/ up to 2% carbon.
  • Sterling silver is an alloy (मिश्र धातु) of silver that consists of 92.5% pure silver and 7.5% of other metal usually copper. Sterling silver is used in jewellery, silverware and decorations. The addition of copper to silver increases the hardness of the alloy without any significant loss of lustre or colour. List of some alloys, their components and uses S.N. Name of Alloy Components Uses
  • Diamond is a metastable allotrope of carbon where the carbon atoms are arranged in a variation of the face-centered cubic crystal structure called a diamond lattice. Marble is a metamorphic rock composed mainly of crystalline calcium carbonate or calcium magnesium carbonate. The most common component of sand is silicon dioxide in the form of quartz...
  • Water has a defined density (घनत्व) (1 gram per cubic centimetre) while the lightest metals are lighter than water. They are Lithium 0.53 gm/cm 3, Potassium 0.862 g/cm 3 and Sodium 0.971 g/cm3. These are malleable and highly reactive so they are impractical to use as the basis of an alloy (मिश्र धातु) with any structural utility.
  • An amalgam is a substance formed by the reaction of Mercury with another metal.
  • The common composition of solder is around 32% Tin and 68% Lead. This combination has a low melting point and is useful for soldering components that are sensitive to heat (ऊष्मा).
  • Bronze is an alloy (मिश्र धातु) made up of Copper and another metal Tin. Compositions may vary but most modern bronze is around 88% Copper and 12% Tin.

Alloys: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • The general properties of non-metals are – (i) High ionization energies. (ii) High electronegativities. (iii) Poor thermal conductors (Graphite is an exception). (iv) Poor electrical conductors (Graphite is an exception). (v) Brittle solids-not malleable or ductile. (vi) Little or no metallic luster (Iodine is an exception).. (vii) Generally gain electrons easily (except the noble gases). (viii) Generally dull, although they may be colourful. (ix) These are generally gases as – Hydrogen, Oxygen, Fluorine, Chlorine, Argon, Krypton, etc.; solids as - Carbon, Phosphorus, Sulfur, Selenium, Iodine, etc.; and liquid – Bromine (only).   • An oxocarbon is an inorganic compound (यौगिक), consisting wholly of carbon and oxygen.
  • The simplest and most common oxocarbons are carbon monoxide (CO) and carbon dioxide (CO2). Carbon has three main allotropes : (i) Graphite (ii) Diamond (iii) Buckminsterfullerene
  • Graphene, Q-carbon, amorphous carbon etc. are some other allotropes of carbon. Graphite :

Solder of Tin and Lead is known as soft solder. The chemical name of bleaching powder is calcium hypochlorite Ca(ClO)2. Sodium Thiosulphate (Hypo) is a crystalline white smellless solid.

Like all radioactive isotopes tritium decays. As it decays it gives off or emits beta radiation (विकिरण).

  • It is the oxide of deuterium.
  • It was discovered by Harold C. Urey in 1932, while G.N. Lewis and R.T. MacDonald were able to isolate the first sample of practically pure heavy water (D2O) in 1933.
  • It is a valuable substance. Its uses are as follows– (i) As neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) moderator – The substance which moderates the fast moving neutrons in atomic reactors is termed as moderator. Heavy water is used as a neutron moderator in atomic reactors. It is also used as a coolant in reactors. (ii) It is used to prepare deuterium and deuterium compounds. Effect of Heavy Water on livings – (i) Intake of high concentration of heavy water can be toxic. It reacts more slowly than ordinary water, as a result the physiological reactions of the body become abnormal. (ii) It inhibits the growth of plants. (iii) Seed germination inhibited in the presence of high D2O. Manufacturing of Heavy water in India –
  • The first heavy water plant was set up in India at Nangal (Punjab) in 1962, which was later closed down. Other heavy water plants were established at Baroda (Gujarat), Tuticorin (Tamil Nadu), Kota (Rajasthan), Hazira (Gujarat), Manuguru (Telangana), Thal (Maharashtra) and Talcher (Odisha). Among them, production of heavy water at Baroda and Talcher was suspended later. Soft and Hard Water : Soft water –
  • Coal minerals are divided mainly into four types on the basis of the percentage of Carbon content – Lignite – 25-35% Sub-bituminous – 35-45% Bituminous – 45-86% Anthracite – 86-97%.
  • G–368 General Studies General Science Pig Iron has a very high Carbon content, typically 3.5–4.5%. Wrought Iron is an Iron alloy (मिश्र धातु) with a very low Carbon (less than 0.08%) content. Steels containing about 0.2% to 1.5% Carbon are generally known as Carbon steel. The amount of Carbon in alloy steel ranges from about 0.1% to 1%.
  • The carat (ct) is a unit (मात्रक) of mass (द्रव्यमान) equal to 200 mg and is used for measuring gemstones and pearls.

Hydrogen and its compounds: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Hydrogen is a chemical element (तत्व) with symbol H, atomic number 1 and its electronic configuration is 1s1.
  • It is the lightest element (तत्व) in the periodic table with a standard atomic weight (भार) of approx. 1.008.
  • It occupies a unique position in the periodic table.
  • In its properties, it behaves like alkali metals (Li, Na, K etc.) as well as halogens (F, Cl, Br etc.). Isotopes (समस्थानिक) of Hydrogen :
  • There are three naturally occuring isotopes (समस्थानिक) of hydrogen – (i) Protium - 1H1 (A = 1) Without any neutron (न्यूट्रॉन) – stable form (ii) Deuterium - 1H2 (A = 2) With one neutron – stable form (iii) Tritium - 1H3 (A = 3) With two neutrons – it is unstable and therefore it is radioactive. Like all radioactive isotopes tritium decays. As it decays it gives off or emits beta radiation (विकिरण).
  • Deuterium was discovered (1931) by American chemist- Harold C. Urey (for which he was awarded the Nobel Prize for Chemistry in 1934) and his associates F. Brickwedde and G. Murphy.
  • In nature, triti um is found in very less amount. It is prepared in atomic reactors by artificial techniques.
  • The existence of tritium was established by Bleakney and Gould in 1934 during mass (द्रव्यमान) spectrograph analysis of heavy water. Tritium was first detected in 1934 by Ernest Rutherford, M.L. Oliphant and Paul Harteck – when they bombarded deuterium with high energy (ऊर्जा) deuterons (nuclei of deuterium atoms). Properties of Deuterium :
  • Hydrogen is the purest combustion fuel. Water is generated from burning of hydrogen. While coal, kerosene oil and diesel are known as fossil fuel or carbonic fuel which generates carbon dioxide and many other harmful gases when it burns.
  • & (b) Heavy water (D2O) or deuterium oxide is used as a moderator as well as a coolant in nuclear reactors because it slows down neutrons effectively and also has a low probability of absorption of neutrons. Acid (अम्ल), Base (क्षार) and Salt (लवण): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • The acid (अम्ल) name comes from Latin acidus which means sour and refers to the sharp odour and sour taste of many acids.
  • According to Arrhenius, acid (अम्ल) is a compound (यौगिक) that can increase the hydrogen-ion (H +/Hydronium-ion H 3O+) concentration in aqueous solution.
  • According to Bronsted - Lowry concept, acid (अम्ल) is defined as any hydrogen containing material which can donate a proton (प्रोटॉन) (hydron) to other substances. Properties of Acids : (i) Acids in solution have a pH below 7.0 (at about 25°C). (ii) Taste is sour. (iii) Releases hydrogen-ion (H+) in water. (iv) An acid turns blue litmus paper into red. (v) Chemicals or substances having the property of an acid are said to be acidic. Classification of acids :
  • Acids are divided into two main classes : I. Strong acids : These are completely ionized in water. They are very corrosive and can cause severe skin burns. Examples are hydrochloric acid (अम्ल) (HCl), Sulfuric acid (H2SO4) and nitric acid (HNO3). II. Weak acids : These are partially ionized in water. They are mildly corrosive and normally do not affect the skin. Examples are citric acid, acetic acid, tartaric acid etc. Sources of Some Natural Acids S.N. Name of Acid Source 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 7. 8. Tartaric Acid Acetic acid (Ethanoic acid) Citric acid Oxalic acid Lactic acid Malic acid Ascorbic acid (Vitamin (विटामिन) C) Formic acid Grapes, Tamarind, Some citrus fruits, Bananas Vinegar Lemon, Orange and commonly in all citrus fruits Tomato, Spinach Curd Apple Amla, Guava, Papaya, Orange Stings of ants and Stinging Nettle (a plant) Uses of Acids : (i) Tartaric acid, lactic acid, acetic acid and malic acid are edible acids. (ii) Oxalic acid is used to remove the rust/iron stains on clothes. (iii) Conc.-Nitric acid is used in the preparation of explosive substances and for seperation of silver from gold-silver alloys. (iv) Aqua regia is a mixture (मिश्रण) of three-part of conc-hydrochloric acid and one part of conc-nitric acid (3 conc-HCl : 1 conc- HNO3). It has capacity to dissolve platinum and gold. It is also known as Royal water. It is primarily used to produce chloroauric acid for refining the highest quality of gold. It is also used in laboratories to remove trace amounts of organic compounds and trace metals from glassware. (v) Carbonic acid is (through carbonation of water from added CO2) present in soda and many soft drinks. (vi) Phosphoric acid is used as acidulant in Coca-Cola. (vii) Certain acids are used as drugs. Acetylsalicylic acid (Aspirin) is used as a pain-killer and for bringing down fever. Role of Acids in Human Body :
  • Acids play important roles in the human body. (i) The hydrochloric acid (अम्ल) present in the stomach (आमाशय) helps in the digestion (पाचन). (ii) Amino acids are the structural unit (मात्रक) of protein. For the synthesis of protein, amino acids are required. Protein is very essential for the growth and repair of body cells. (iii) Fatty acids are also required for growth and repair of body tissue. (iv) Nucleic acids (DNA and RNA) are essential for all known forms of life. Genes/DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) are responsible for transmitting of traits to offsprings from parents; RNA (ribonucleic acid) is involved in protein synthesis. (v) The carbonic acid/bicarbonate (H 2CO3/ HCO3
  • ) buffer system is essential for the maintenance of pH equilibrium in the body. Base (क्षार) :
  • According to Arrhenius , a base (क्षार) is a compound (यौगिक) that increases the concentration of hydroxide ions (OH –) in aqueous solution.
  • According to Bronsted-Lowry concept, base (क्षार) is any substance that can accept a proton (प्रोटॉन) (hydron) from an acid (अम्ल)/ proton donor. Properties of Bases : (i) Bases have a bitter taste. (ii) Bases feel slippery. (iii) Bases conduct electricity in aqueous solution, because they consist of charged particles in solution. G–382 General Studies General Science (iv) A water-soluble base is called an alkali if it produces OH– ions in water. (v) Bases in solution have a pH above 7.0 (at about 25°C). (vi) An alkali turns red litmus into blue. Some bases and their uses are as follows : (a) Calcium hydroxide Ca(OH)2–Slaked Lime (Limewater is saturated aqueous solution of this) (b) Ammonium hydroxide NH 4OH (Aqueous ammonia)–to clean glass windows. (c) Sodium hydroxide NaOH–used to prepare soap. (d) Potassium hydroxide KOH – used to prepare soap. (e) Magnesium hydroxide Mg(OH)2 – Its aqueous suspension is known as milk of magnesia, a common antacid. It is used to remove the acidity of the stomach (आमाशय) and also as a laxative. pH Scale :
  • Aqua regia (Latin : royal water ) is a highly powerful acidic, corrosive and oxidizing mixture (मिश्रण) of usually three parts concentrated hydrochloric acid (अम्ल) (HCl) and one part concentrated nitric acid (HNO 3). It is called aqua regia because it is one of the few acid mixtures that can dissolve the 'noble' (chemically generally inert) metals like Gold (Au),...
  • On the pH scale (at 25°C), pure water is neutral with pH 7. An acidic solution has pH less than 7, whereas a basic (alkaline) solution has pH greater than 7. Therefore, both the given statements are incorrect.

Acid (अम्ल), Base (क्षार) and Salt (लवण): Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • A pH value is a number generally from 0 to 14 with 7 as the middle (neutral) point (temperature (तापमान)-dependent). Values below 7 indicate acidic nature which increases as the number decreases while values above 7 indicate alkaline/ basic nature which increases as the number increases.

  • Pure water is neutral. Its pH value is 7 (at about 25°C). Chemical Indicator :

Acid (अम्ल), Base (क्षार) and Salt (लवण): Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • In organic chemistry, a hydrocarbon (हाइड्रोकार्बन) is an organic compound (यौगिक) consisting entirely of hydrogen and carbon.
  • Hydrocarbons from which one hydrogen atom (परमाणु) has been removed are univalent substituent groups called hydrocarbyls, e.g. Methyl, phenyl etc.
  • In hydrocarbons the carbon atoms join together to form the framework of the compound (यौगिक) and the hydrogen atoms attach to them in many different configurations.
  • Hydrocarbons are the principal constituents of petroleum and natural gas.
  • Hydrocarbons serve as fuels and lubricants as well as raw materials for the production of plastics, fibres, rubbers, solvents, explosives and industrial chemicals.
  • Many hydrocarbons occur in nature. In addition to making up fossil fuels, they are present in trees and plants. For example in the form of pigments called carotene (C40Hx e.g. β-carotene C40H56) that occur in carrots and green leaves.
  • Compressed natural gas (CNG) is natural gas that is compressed; ‘liquefied’ petroleum gas (LPG) is derived from crude oil refining or natural gas processing; many domestic and automobile fuels are refined from crude oil.
  • Besides above substances, diesel, kerosene, lubricants, paraffin waxes are also obtained by the fractional distillation of petroleum. Classification of Hydrocarbons :
  • Ethylene is used in artificial fruit ripening, carbon dioxide is used in soft drinks, baking soda is the commercial name of sodium bicarbonate and quartz is used for producing sodium silicate.
  • All the four given salts can be used as table salt (लवण). Sea salt, rock salt and table salt contain around 100% sodium chloride which means that too much of any of them will have a negative effect on your health. However, rock salt and sea salt are often marketed as being a healthier or tastier option than table salt and can be considerably more expensi...

Acid (अम्ल), Base (क्षार) and Salt (लवण): Advanced Observations & Traps

  • The hydrocarbons, which contain carbon atoms linked on the non-cyclic (acyclic) open-chain are known as open- chain hydrocarbons. In these hydrocarbons, the chain of carbon atoms is not cyclic.
  • Open-chain hydrocarbons are further divided into (i) Saturated hydrocarbons (ii) Unsaturated hydrocarbons (i) Saturated hydrocarbons (Alkanes):
  • Those hydrocarbons, in which the carbon atoms are bonded to each other with a single covalent bond (–C–C–) are called a cyclic saturated hydrocarbons. They are also known as alkanes.
  • They follow the general formula CnH2n+2 (–ane suffix). For example-methane, ethane, propane, etc.
  • They are also known as paraffin because these are less reactive in normal conditions with acids and other reagents (Parum = too little, Affinis = related → little affinity). Methane (CH4) :
  • Methane is the first member of open chain saturated hydrocarbons.
  • Methane is the primary constituent of natural gas and it is released into the atmosphere from crude oil production and some industrial activities. More of it is released from biological processes. Methane is produced primarily by methanogenic archaea (methanogens) under anaerobic conditions; in termites and ruminants (like cows), methane is produced by gut/rumen methanogens.
  • It is also known as Marsh gas due to its production in the wetland by decomposition of organic materials by anaerobic archaea (methanogens).   • Sodium thiosulphate (Hypo) is used in photography as a fixer of positive and negative. It is also used in the extraction of Gold and Silver.

Hydrocarbons: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • In organic chemistry, a hydrocarbon (हाइड्रोकार्बन) is an organic compound (यौगिक) consisting entirely of hydrogen and carbon.
  • Hydrocarbons from which one hydrogen atom (परमाणु) has been removed are univalent substituent groups called hydrocarbyls, e.g. Methyl, phenyl etc.
  • In hydrocarbons the carbon atoms join together to form the framework of the compound (यौगिक) and the hydrogen atoms attach to them in many different configurations.
  • Hydrocarbons are the principal constituents of petroleum and natural gas.
  • Hydrocarbons serve as fuels and lubricants as well as raw materials for the production of plastics, fibres, rubbers, solvents, explosives and industrial chemicals.
  • Many hydrocarbons occur in nature. In addition to making up fossil fuels, they are present in trees and plants. For example in the form of pigments called carotene (C40Hx e.g. β-carotene C40H56) that occur in carrots and green leaves.
  • Compressed natural gas (CNG) is natural gas that is compressed; ‘liquefied’ petroleum gas (LPG) is derived from crude oil refining or natural gas processing; many domestic and automobile fuels are refined from crude oil.
  • Besides above substances, diesel, kerosene, lubricants, paraffin waxes are also obtained by the fractional distillation of petroleum. Classification of Hydrocarbons :
  • Carbon is the fundamental element (तत्व) of all organic compounds (including bio-compounds). The organic compounds are a large class of chemical compounds in which one or more atoms of carbon are covalently linked to atoms of other elements, most commonly hydrogen, oxygen or nitrogen.
  • The Bhopal disaster or the Bhopal Gas Tragedy was a disaster that resulted from an accident. It happened at a Union Carbide subsidiary pesticide plant in Bhopal, India. On the night of 2-3 December 1984, the plant released 42 tonnes of toxic methyl isocyanate (MIC) gas, exposing more than 500,000 people to toxic gases.

  • It was historically used as an anaesthetic.
  • It is widely used to manufacture polythene.

  • Mustard gas is a warfare agent known as the Sulfur mustard (HD), which can form blisters on exposed skin and in the lungs (फेफड़े). (b) Alkynes :
  • Acyclic hydrocarbons containing a triple bond between carbon atoms are known as an alkyne.

Chapter 28

Alcohol

Comprehensive short notes on Alcohol (Chemistry) with key concepts, definitions, and real UPPSC previous year questions.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Beer is usually derived from the fermentation of malt derived from the digestion of germinated barley grains. Barley, water, hops and yeast ...RO/ARO Mains ’161 paper
  2. Methyl alcohol (CH 3OH) is also called wood spirit. It is obtained by the destructive distillation of wood.PCS Mains ’151 paper

Alcohol: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Alcohol is an organic compound (यौगिक) with a hydroxyl (–OH) functional group attached to a saturated carbon atom (परमाणु). Because –OH is the functional group of all alcohols, we often represent alcohols by the general formula R-OH where R is an alkyl group.
  • In other words, the hydroxyl derivatives of alkenes/ aliphatic hydrocarbons are termed as alcohols.
  • Condensed/structural formula and name of some alcohols are as follows : (i) Methyl Alcohol (Methanol) – CH3OH (ii) Ethyl Alcohol (Ethanol) – C2H5OH (iii) n-Propyl Alcohol – CH3CH2CH2OH (iv) Isopropyl Alcohol – (CH3)2CHOH (v) n-Butyl Alcohol – CH3 (CH2)2CH2OH (vi) Isobutyl Alcohol – (CH3)2CHCH2OH Methyl Alcohol (Methanol or Carbinol) :
  • Its chemical formula is CH3OH.
  • It acquired the name wood alcohol or wood naphtha because it was once produced primarily by the destructive distillation of wood.
  • Today it is mainly produced by hydrogenation of carbon monoxide.
  • It is the simplest alcohol, consisting of a methyl group linked to a hydroxyl group.
  • It is light (प्रकाश), volatile, colourless, flammable liquid with a distinctive odour similar to that of ethanol (Ethyl alcohol).
  • Methyl alcohol (CH 3OH) is also called wood spirit. It is obtained by the destructive distillation of wood.

Alcohol: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • It is used in the preparation of formaldehyde, formic acid (अम्ल), acetic acid, methyl chloride, methyl salicylate, etc.
  • It is used in the preparation of the methylated spirit. A mixture (मिश्रण) of methyl alcohol and ethyl alcohol is known as methylated spirit or denatured alcohol, which is not suitable for drinking.
  • The mixture (मिश्रण) of methyl alcohol (40%) and water (60%) is used earlier in radiators of automobiles as antifreeze . Modern automobile engine coolants are predominantly ethylene-glycol based. Ethyl Alcohol (Ethanol) :
  • Ethyl alcohol (C2H5OH) is commonly called alcohol.
  • It is found in forms of ethyl esters of carboxylic acids in fruits, plants and fragrant oils.
  • It is the primary constituent of all types of wines, so it is called the spirits of wines.
  • It is generally produced by fermentation of carbohydrates by yeast, so it is also called grain alcohol. Absolute Alcohol–
  • Absolute alcohol is specified as ≥ 99.5% (v/v) ethanol.
  • Beer is usually derived from the fermentation of malt derived from the digestion (पाचन) of germinated barley grains. Barley, water, hops and yeast are the four magic ingredients that are required for making beer. Other grains like maize, rice, rye and wheat are also used in making beer. Whisky is also made from fermented barley grains.

Polymer (बहुलक): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

The reactants (i.e. the small molecules from which the polymer (बहुलक) is constructed) are called monomers and products of the polymerization process are called polymers. Organic Acid (अम्ल): Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • A carboxylic acid (अम्ल) is an organic compound (यौगिक) that contains a functional carboxyl group (C(=O)OH).
  • The general formula of a carboxylic acid (अम्ल) is R-COOH with R referring to the rest of the molecule (अणु).
  • Carboxylic acids occur widely and include the amino acids (which make up proteins) and acetic acid (अम्ल) (which is part of vinegar).
  • Salts and esters of carboxylic acids are called carboxylates.
  • On basis of the presence of carboxyl group in acid (अम्ल), carboxylic acids have been divided into three main types–
  • An explosive is a chemical compound (यौगिक)/mixture (मिश्रण)/device intended to function by explosion and capable (by chemical reaction) of producing rapidly expanding gases; initiation can involve fire, friction, concussion, percussion, etc.
  • Explosives contain highly reactive chemicals that are full of potential energy (ऊर्जा), which can be released with a bang.
  • A good explosive should contains following properties– (i) Explosive should not be volatile. (ii) Explosive generally should not be hygroscopic. (iii) Explosive should be stable.
  • Polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE) is usually known by its brand name Teflon. PTFE is a solid fluorocarbon. Its density (घनत्व) is 2.2 g/cm3 and its melting point is about 327oC. This is especially used for making a non-stick surface coating for utensils.

  • This is a yellow solid material.

  • Amatol is an explosive made by mixing TNT with ammonium nitrate. Trinitroglycerine (TNG) :
  • It is a dense, colourless, oily, explosive liquid most commonly produced by nitrating glycerol with white fuming nitric acid (अम्ल) in presence of Sulfuric acid.
  • It is also known as nitroglycerine or Nobel oil.

Explosive Substances: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • An explosive is a chemical compound (यौगिक)/mixture (मिश्रण)/device intended to function by explosion and capable (by chemical reaction) of producing rapidly expanding gases; initiation can involve fire, friction, concussion, percussion, etc.
  • Explosives contain highly reactive chemicals that are full of potential energy (ऊर्जा), which can be released with a bang.
  • A good explosive should contains following properties– (i) Explosive should not be volatile. (ii) Explosive generally should not be hygroscopic. (iii) Explosive should be stable.
  • Explosive reaction may be detonation (high explosives) or deflagration/burning (low explosives).
  • Some explosives are as follows– Trinitrotoluene (TNT) :
  • TNT is made by combining toluene with a mixture (मिश्रण) of nitric acid (अम्ल) and sulfuric acid.
  • It is a chemical compound (यौगिक) with the formula C 6H2(NO2)3 CH3.
  • This is a yellow solid material.
  • Nitrochloroform, also known as chloropicrin, is a chemical compound (यौगिक) currently used as a broad-spectrum antimicrobial, fungicide, herbicide, and insecticide. It is also used as tear gas. It was manufactured for use as poison gas in World War I. Other options are explosives.
  • Trinitroglycerin (TNG) also known as Nobel oil, is an explosive fluid. Chemically, it is an organic nitrate compound (यौगिक).

Explosive Substances: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • It is prepared via multi-step routes e.g., involving sulfonation of phenol followed by nitration.
  • It is used as a laboratory reagent as well as an explosive.
  • It has also been used in medicine (antiseptic, burn treatments) and dyes.
  • It is a light (प्रकाश) yellow, crystalline solid. Dynamite :
  • It was invented by Alfred Nobel in 1867.
  • It is made by absorbing nitroglycerine in sawdust or kieselguhr (diatomaceous earth also known as D.E. or a naturally occurring soft, siliceous sedimentary rock that is easily crumbled into a fine white powder).
  • Dynamite is mainly used in the mining, quarrying (excavation), construction and demolition industries. Blasting Gelatin :
  • Blasting gelatin is a mixture (मिश्रण) of 6-8% nitrocellulose, 91- 93% of nitroglycerine and bout 1% of an acid (अम्ल) acceptor.

It is also known as cyclonite, hexogen and T4. It is more powerful explosive than TNT. Due to its high explosive property, it was widely used in Second World War. RDX is a co...
  • Cordite, blasting gelatin and dynamite contain nitroglycerin as an essential constituent, while a highly explosive element (तत्व) is Amatol. It is composed of ammonium nitrate and TNT (Trinitrotoluene) in varying ratios.

Fuel: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • A fuel is any material that can be made to react with other substances so that it releases energy (ऊर्जा) as heat (ऊष्मा) energy or to be used for work (कार्य). Some common fuels are wood, coal, petrol, diesel etc. Uranium and Plutonium are nuclear fuels used to produce nuclear energy. Ideal fuel :
  • Cordite, blasting gelatin and dynamite contain nitroglycerin as an essential constituent, while a highly explosive element (तत्व) is Amatol. It is composed of ammonium nitrate and TNT (Trinitrotoluene) in varying ratios.
  • Glycerol is an organic compound (यौगिक). Reacting with conc. HNO3 and conc. H2SO4, it produces nitroglycerin. Nitroglycerin is a heavy, colourless, oily, explosive liquid. It is also called trinitroglycerin (TNG), glyceryl trinitrate (GTN) or Nobel’s oil. It is mainly used in the explosives such as dynamite.
  • The petroleum products are subject to natural fuel. They are made from the fossils buried within the Earth. Other options coke and tar can be achieved by destructive distillation of coal or wood, while coal gas can be created artificially.
  • The use of natural gas in power (शक्ति) generation provides a cleaner alternative to coal and other fossil fuels, reducing carbon and other emissions and resulting in both immediate and long- term benefits for public health and the environment.
  • Fossil fuels, including coal, oil and natural gas are currently the world’s primary energy (ऊर्जा) sources. The fossil fuels formed from the organic material over the course of million of years have fuelled the global economy over the past century. Yet the fossil fuels are finite resources and they can also irreparably harm the environment.
  • The octane number of a fuel (petrol) is the measure of its antiknock quality when it is used in a spark ignition, internal combustion engine as compared to the antiknock quality of ISO-octane. The higher octane number of a fuel means the better anti-knocking capacity for that fuel.
  • G–418 General Studies General Science Tetraethyl Lead (CH3CH2)4Pb is the chief anti-knock agent for automotive gasoline or petrol. In the hot cylinder of a gasoline engine, the bonds between the lead atom (परमाणु) and the ethyl groups are broken. Upon combustion, the lead atom forms lead oxide (PbO) which prevents fractions of the fuel mixture (मिश्रण) from burning...

Fuel: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • An ideal fuel is a fuel which produces adequate amount of heat (ऊष्मा) and very less amount of pollutants.
  • As the octane number is used to measure the ignition stability of gasoline (petrol), the cetane number is used as a quality parameter of diesel. The combustion of diesel without spark is provided by compression. Thus as soon as the diesel fuel is ignited and compressed the cetane number will be higher. This identifies the good quality of diesel.
  • A diesel engine is an internal combustion engine in which ignition in fuel is generated by using heat (ऊष्मा) produced by compressing air in closed place. There is no carbureter in diesel engines for combustion as in petrol engines. In a diesel engine, the air inside a cylinder is compressed to such a level so that the temperature (तापमान) of air reaches around 80 ...
  • Methane is the principal gas in biogas. It is also the main component of natural gas, a fossil fuel, and can be used to replace natural gas in many applications including cooking, heating, steam production, etc.
  • Biogas typically refers to a mixture (मिश्रण) of different gases produced by the breakdown of organic matter in the absence of oxygen (anaerobic decomposition). Biogas is primarily methane and carbon dioxide and may have small amounts of hydrogen sulfide (H2S), water vapour (moisture), nitrogen and siloxanes. (e) It can transport easily. (f) It has high calorific value. (g) It does not leave behind any undesirable substances. Heating value of fuel :
  • Rice paddies are a significant source of methane (CH 4) a potent greenhouse gas. They also emit small amounts of nitrous oxide (N2O).

Food Preservation, Nutrition, Medicine, etc.: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Food preservation may be defined as the process of treating and handling food in such a way as to stop, control or greatly slow down spoilage and, of course, to minimize the possibility of foodborne illness, while maintaining the optimum nutritional value, texture and flavour. Principles of Food Preservation :
  • The principles of food preservation are as follows– Detergent :
  • Detergent is a water soluble cleansing surfactant (or a mixture (मिश्रण) containing one or more surfactants) which removes oily dirt by emulsifying/entrapping it is micelles and keeping it dispersed/suspended in water. It differs from soap as it does not form a scum with the salts in hard water.
  • 'Hydrocarbon (हाइड्रोकार्बन) Vision 2025' was associated with storage of petroleum products and focus on long-term energy (ऊर्जा) security. Detergents and Soap: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Detergent is a water soluble cleansing surfactant (or a mixture (मिश्रण) containing one or more surfactants) which removes oily dirt by emulsifying/entrapping it is micelles and keeping it dispersed/suspended in water. It differs from soap as it does not form a scum with the salts in hard water.
  • Detergent helps to remove dirt and grease from porous surfaces such as fabrics, clothes, non-treated wood and/ or non-porous surfaces such as metals, plastics and treated wood.
  • Soap is used preferably for soft water but detergent can be used for soft water as well as hard water, because the calcium and magnesium salts of detergents are soluble in water.
  • Chemically detergents include ionic, non-ionic and zwitterionic surfactants with higher molecular weight (भार), whose anion or cation has a long chain of carbon atoms from 12 to 18.
  • For example– Sodium Lauryl Sulphate is an anionic surfactant with a long chain of 12 carbon atoms.
  • The aqueous solution of detergent is usually neutral so it is used to clean the clothes made from delicate fibres.
  • The solution of soap is alkaline due to hydrolysis, so it is harmful for cleaning delicate clothes. Soap :
  • Soaps are salts of fatty acids whereas fatty acids are aliphatic monocarboxylic acids (saturated or unsaturated) that have long carbon chains e.g. palmitic acid (अम्ल) (C15H31COOH) and stearic acid (C17H35COOH).
  • Eugenol is a colourless or light (प्रकाश) yellow aromatic oily liquid extracted from cloves having chemical formula C 10H12O2. It smells like cloves with spicy pungent taste. It is the main component of oil of clove.
  • The smaller acid (अम्ल) fruits are more suitable for jelly making since they are usually high in pectin content and acid. Guavas have high calcium and phosphorus contents. High pectin contents make guava suitable for jelly making.

Detergents and Soap: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Fertilizers are used by farmers and families to help crops and gardens grow.
  • With the global population steadily growing, it is necessary that enough crops are produced each year to provide adequate food, clothing and other agricultural products to people around the world. These crops deplete soil nutrients in different ways and rates.
  • Fertilizers play an important role in providing crops with the nutrients they need to grow and be harvested for nutritious food. Manure :
  • Manure is an organic material that is used to fertilize land, usually consisting of the feces and urine of domestic animals, with or without accompanying litter such as straw, hay, etc.
  • Animals manure is less rich in nitrogen, phosphorus and potash than synthetic fertilizers and hence must be applied in much greater quantities than the latter.
  • Manure is rich in organic matter and improves the soil's capacity to absorb water and store water, thus helping reduce runoff and erosion.
  • Much of the potassium and nitrogen in manure can be lost through leaching, if the material is exposed to rainfall before being applied to the field. These nutrient losses may be minimized/reduced by such methods as stacking manure under cover or in pits to prevent leaching.
  • Compost is a managed aerobic decomposition product of manure.
  • Organic food is produced by farmers who emphasize the use of renewable resources and the conservation of soil and water to enhance environmental quality for future generations. The organic food is produced without using most conventional pesticides; fertilizers made with synthetic ingredients or sewage sludge, bioengineering or ionizing radiation (विकिरण). ...

Fertilizers: Fundamental Principles & Definitions

  • Fertilizer (उर्वरक) is any material of natural or synthetic origin that is applied to soils or to plant tissues to supply one or more plant nutrients essential to the growth of plants.
  • Fertilizers are used by farmers and families to help crops and gardens grow.
  • With the global population steadily growing, it is necessary that enough crops are produced each year to provide adequate food, clothing and other agricultural products to people around the world. These crops deplete soil nutrients in different ways and rates.
  • Fertilizers play an important role in providing crops with the nutrients they need to grow and be harvested for nutritious food. Manure :
  • Manure is an organic material that is used to fertilize land, usually consisting of the feces and urine of domestic animals, with or without accompanying litter such as straw, hay, etc.
  • Animals manure is less rich in nitrogen, phosphorus and potash than synthetic fertilizers and hence must be applied in much greater quantities than the latter.
  • The fertilizer (उर्वरक) is a substance added to soil to improve plant growth and yield. Nitrogen, Phosphorus and Potassium (N,P,K) are necessary elements of plants. Only 1.5 to 3 kg of Phosphorus is obtained from 1 metric ton of an organic manure. Thus, chemical fertilizers are necessary for the phosphorus need of plants.
  • The fertilizer (उर्वरक) is a chemical or natural substance added to the soil to increase its fertility. There are at least 16 elements essential for the growth of the plant. Carbon, Hydrogen and Oxygen are derived from carbon dioxide and water. Nitrogen, Phosphorous, Potassium, Calcium, Magnesium, Boron, Chlorine, Copper, Iron, Manganese, Molybdenum and Zin...
  • Nitrogen, Phosphorus and Potassium are macro-elements of the plants. Nitrogen deficiency causes the yellowing of leaves and retarded growth and development of plants. Plants absorb phosphorus as phosphate ions. It favours the healthy root growth by helping translocation of food. Potassium plays an important role in the opening and closing of stomat...
  • The soil acidic causes a reduction (अपचयन) in the yields of acid (अम्ल)- sensitive crops. The acidic soils are readily identified by a soil test showing a low pH value. The soil reaction is alkaline when the pH value is above 7, neutral at 7 and acidic below 7 (at 25°C temp.). The extensive use of ammonium sulphate is not recommended on acidic soils because of it...

Fertilizers: Applications, Laws & Formulae

  • Manure is rich in organic matter and improves the soil's capacity to absorb water and store water, thus helping reduce runoff and erosion.
  • Much of the potassium and nitrogen in manure can be lost through leaching, if the material is exposed to rainfall before being applied to the field. These nutrient losses may be minimized/reduced by such methods as stacking manure under cover or in pits to prevent leaching.
  • Compost is a managed aerobic decomposition product of manure.
  • Green manure is a type of c over crop, grown to add nutrients and organic matter to the soil. Typically, a green manure crop is grown for a specific period of time, and then ploughed under and incorporated into the soil while it is green or shortly after flowering.
  • The legumes (mainly pea family plants) used as green manuring crops have nitrogen fixing symbiotic bacteria (जीवाणु) in their root nodules that fix atmospheric nitrogen in a form that plant can use as well as providing organic matter (humus) to the soil. Such plants are clover and vetch.
  • Continuous use of chemical fertilizers on soil can destroy soil fertility because the organic matter in the soil is not replenished. As a result, the food produced in these soils may have less vitamins and nutrient contents. The number of microorganisms in soil may decrease. Repeated ammonium-based nitrogen fertilizers may acidify soil. So the application of organic manure has been recommended along with artificial manure. Vermicompost :
  • Urea is an organic compound (यौगिक) whose chemical formula is CO(NH2)2. Nearly 46% of nitrogen in the urea is present in the form of amide. In the field of organic chemicals it is also called carbamide.
  • Urea or Carbamide is an organic compound (यौगिक) with a chemical formula CO(NH2)2 having about 46% nitrogen approximately. The urea molecule (अणु) has two amide (NH2) groups containing nitrogen. Hence, option (c) is the correct answer.
  • Urea is an organic compound (यौगिक) that is only stable in an acidic environment. It is named after the Greek word 'Oura' meaning urine. Urea is very easily soluble in water as compared to other fertilizers.

Fertilizers: Analytical & Comparative Facts

  • Nowadays vermicompost is widely used as an organic soil amendment. The most significant benefit of vermicompost is that the nutrients in it are generally very easily absorbed by the roots of plants. Vermicompost is not easily flushed from the soil because of the worm mucus that it contains. Plants have longer time to obtain the nutrients and get the maximum benefit. Difference between Synthetic Fertilizer (उर्वरक) and Manure S.N. Synthetic Fertilizer Manure
  • Allethrin is a pyrethroid (type I) insecticide. A pyrethroid is a synthetic chemical compound (यौगिक) similar to the natural chemical pyrethrins. Pyrethrins are natural, fast-acting insecticides derived from chrysanthemum flowers (Tanacetum cinerariifolium) that act as neurotoxins to insects. Pyrethroid insecticide are used against mosquitoes.
  • A fire extinguisher is an active fire protection device used to extinguish to control fires, often in emergency situations. In the soda-ice fire extinguisher carbon dioxide is produced by the reaction of sodium bicarbonate and conc. Sulfuric acid (अम्ल). 2NaHCO 3 + H2SO4 → Na2SO4 + 2H2O + 2CO2
  • Rodenticides are pesticides that kill rodents. It is also called rat poison. Rodenticides are made from the chemicals such as warfarin or zinc phosphide. These are very poisonous chemicals.
  • Aluminium phosphide is a cheap, easily available agricultural pesticide which causes lethal poisoning by liberation of phosphine gas which causes cell (कोशिका) hypoxia due to inhibition of oxidative phosphorylation, leading to circulatory failure. Aluminium phosphide is also used as fumigant for preservation of cereals.
  • Mustard Gas is the prototypical substance of the Sulfur- based family of cytotoxic and vesicant chemical warfare agents known as the sulfur mustards. Mustard gas can form large blisters on exposed skin and cause severe, delayed burns/injury to the respiratory tract. Mustard gas may be fatal in large exposures and affects extremely to the cells of skin, eye, lungs (फेफड़े) and DNA. Mustard gas was used in first World War as chemical weapon. Its chemical name is dichloro diethyl sulphide.
  • We know that amount of nitrogen in urea = about 46% ` Urea required for supplying 46 gm of nitrogen = 100 gm ` Urea required for supplying 1000 gm (1 kg) of nitrogen = 100 46 ×1000 = 2174 gm = 2.2 kg (approx.) Hence, about 2.2 kg of urea will be required for supplying 1 kg of nitrogen.
  • The Diammonium Phosphate (DAP) is used as a fertilizer (उर्वरक). Its formula is (NH4)2HPO4. The average pH in solution is 7.5–8. The typical formulation is 18% N and 46% P2O5.
  • The foliar fertilization means applying nutrients to plant leaves or needles. Urea is commonly used for foliar fertilization because of its high solubility and its rapid and effective absorption by leaves.
  • Humus usually refers to the natural decay of material such as leaves in the soil's top layer. It is a dark organic material that forms in the soil when plant and animal matter decays.

Chemistry : Miscellaneous: Advanced Observations & Traps

  • The word ‘Science’ comes from the Latin word ‘scientia’, meaning ‘knowledge’.
  • Science is the systematic knowledge of the physical or material world gained through observation and experimentation.
  • Science has been classified into– (1) Natural Sciences and (2) Social Sciences.
  • The natural sciences comprise the physical sciences, Earth and space sciences, and life (biological) sciences; life sciences (biology) are further divided into many branches and sub-branches.
  • Lamarck and Treviranus introduced the term ‘biology’ independently in modern sense in 1802. Branches and Subbranches of Biology Zoology – Detailed study of the members of the animal kingdom and animal life in general. Botany – Detailed study of plants and their life. Palaeontology – The study of history of life on Earth as based on fossils. Fossils are preserved remains, impressions, or traces of plants, animals, and other organisms from the geologic past; they may be preserved in Earth’s crust and also by processes such as drying, freezing, and entrapment in amber or asphalt. Palaeobotany – Study of plant fossils. Ecology – The branch of biology that deals with the relation of organisms to one another and to their physical surroundings. Genetics – The study of heredity (आनुवंशिकता) or how the characteristics of living things are transmitted from one generation to the next. In living organisms, DNA is the genetic material. This material is passed on when organism reproduces. The basic unit (मात्रक) of heredity is ‘Gene (जीन)’. Physiology – Study of normal functions of living creatures and their parts. Pedology – It is the study of soils in their natural environment. It deals with pedogenesis, soil morphology and soil classification. Edaphology – It concerns with the influence of soils on living things, particularly plants. Gerontology – Study of the social, cultural, physiological, cognitive, and biological aspects of ageing, or the scientific study of old age, the process of ageing and the particular problems of old people. Ethology – It is the scientific and objective study of non-human animal behaviour usually with a focus on behaviour under natural conditions, and viewing behaviour as an evolutionary adaptive trait. Bionics – It is the study of mechanical systems that function like living organisms or parts of living organisms. Bionics is the application of biological methods and systems found in nature to the study and design of engineering systems and modern technology. The word ‘bionic’ was coined by Jack E. Steele in 1958. Bionomics – The study of the mode of life of organisms in their natural habitat and their adaptations to their surroundings. Bionomics is the comprehensive study of an organism and its relation to its environment. It is also referred as ‘ecology’. Bionomy – Branch of biology concerned with the laws of life. Biometry – It is also known as biostatistics. It is the development and application of statistical and mathematical methods to analyse the data results from biological observations and phenomenon. Phrenology – The detailed study of the shape and size of the cranium as a supposed indication of character and mental abilities. Anthology – Study of flowers. It is also used for a collection of selected writings/works. Agrostology – It deals with the scientific study of true grasses. It is also called Graminology – scientific study of grasses (the family Poaceae or Gramineae). Palynology – The study of pollen grains and other spores especially as found in archaeological or geological deposits. Pollen extracted from such deposits may be used for radiocarbon dating and for studying past climates and environments by identifying plants then growing. Oncology – A branch of biology (Medicine) that deals with the prevention, diagnosis and treatment of tumours/cancer. Teratology – The scientific study of congenital abnormalities and abnormal formations.
  • Sulfur mustard, commonly known as mustard gas, is a chemical warfare agent which was first used effectively in World War-I by the German army against British and Canadian soldiers near Ypres, Belgium in 1915.
  • Smokescreens generally consist of fine particles of titanium oxides. Titanium dioxide is a naturally occurring oxide of titanium. A smoke screen is a cloud of smoke created to conceal military operations. Zinc chloride is also used in creating smoke screen.

Subdivisions of Biology: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • The word ‘Science’ comes from the Latin word ‘scientia’, meaning ‘knowledge’.
  • Science is the systematic knowledge of the physical or material world gained through observation and experimentation.
  • Science has been classified into– (1) Natural Sciences and (2) Social Sciences.
  • The natural sciences comprise the physical sciences, Earth and space sciences, and life (biological) sciences; life sciences (biology) are further divided into many branches and sub-branches.
  • Zoology is a branch of biology. It deals with the study of both living and extinct animals including their structure, embryology, evolution (जैव विकास), classification, habits, and distribution. Aristotle is known as 'Father of Zoology'.
