Subdivisions of Biology: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Lamarck and Treviranus introduced the term ‘biology’ independently in modern sense in 1802. Branches and Subbranches of Biology Zoology – Detailed study of the members of the animal kingdom and animal life in general. Botany – Detailed study of plants and their life. Palaeontology – The study of history of life on Earth as based on fossils. Fossils are preserved remains, impressions, or traces of plants, animals, and other organisms from the geologic past; they may be preserved in Earth’s crust and also by processes such as drying, freezing, and entrapment in amber or asphalt. Palaeobotany – Study of plant fossils. Ecology – The branch of biology that deals with the relation of organisms to one another and to their physical surroundings. Genetics – The study of heredity (आनुवंशिकता) or how the characteristics of living things are transmitted from one generation to the next. In living organisms, DNA is the genetic material. This material is passed on when organism reproduces. The basic unit (मात्रक) of heredity is ‘Gene (जीन)’. Physiology – Study of normal functions of living creatures and their parts. Pedology – It is the study of soils in their natural environment. It deals with pedogenesis, soil morphology and soil classification. Edaphology – It concerns with the influence of soils on living things, particularly plants. Gerontology – Study of the social, cultural, physiological, cognitive, and biological aspects of ageing, or the scientific study of old age, the process of ageing and the particular problems of old people. Ethology – It is the scientific and objective study of non-human animal behaviour usually with a focus on behaviour under natural conditions, and viewing behaviour as an evolutionary adaptive trait. Bionics – It is the study of mechanical systems that function like living organisms or parts of living organisms. Bionics is the application of biological methods and systems found in nature to the study and design of engineering systems and modern technology. The word ‘bionic’ was coined by Jack E. Steele in 1958. Bionomics – The study of the mode of life of organisms in their natural habitat and their adaptations to their surroundings. Bionomics is the comprehensive study of an organism and its relation to its environment. It is also referred as ‘ecology’. Bionomy – Branch of biology concerned with the laws of life. Biometry – It is also known as biostatistics. It is the development and application of statistical and mathematical methods to analyse the data results from biological observations and phenomenon. Phrenology – The detailed study of the shape and size of the cranium as a supposed indication of character and mental abilities. Anthology – Study of flowers. It is also used for a collection of selected writings/works. Agrostology – It deals with the scientific study of true grasses. It is also called Graminology – scientific study of grasses (the family Poaceae or Gramineae). Palynology – The study of pollen grains and other spores especially as found in archaeological or geological deposits. Pollen extracted from such deposits may be used for radiocarbon dating and for studying past climates and environments by identifying plants then growing. Oncology – A branch of biology (Medicine) that deals with the prevention, diagnosis and treatment of tumours/cancer. Teratology – The scientific study of congenital abnormalities and abnormal formations. Osteology – It is the scientific and detailed study of the bones/bony structure of an organism, skeletal elements, teeth, microbone, morphology etc. Chondrology – A branch of anatomy that deals with cartilage. Serology – The scientific study or diagnostic examination of blood serum, especially with regard to the response of the immune system to pathogens or introduced substances. Pathology – The science of the causes and effects of diseases, especially the branch of medicine that deals with the laboratory examination of samples of body tissue for diagnostic or forensic purposes. Parasitology – Parasitology is the study of parasites (protozoa (प्रोटोजोआ), helminths and ectoparasites), their hosts and the relationship between them. Parasites are organisms that use other species of plants and animals as a host. The hosts provide the environment in which the parasite (परजीवी) lives. Virology – Virology is the study of viruses– submicroscopic, parasitic particles of genetic material contained in a protein coat and virus (विषाणु)-like agents. Bacteriology – Study of the morphology, ecology, genetics and biochemistry of bacteria (जीवाणु) as well as many other aspects related to them. Entomology – A branch of zoology that deals with insects. Malacology – Malacology is the branch of invertebrate zoology that deals with the study of the Mollusca, the second-largest phylum of animals in terms of described species after the arthropods. Molluscs include snails and slugs, clams, octopus, and squid (e.g., colossal squid is often cited as the heaviest invertebrate) and numerous other kinds, many of which have shells made of calcium carbonate. Ichthyology – Ichthyology, also known as fish science, is the branch of zoology that deals with fishes. Serpentology – A branch of zoology that studies snakes. Herpetology – Herpetology is the branch of zoology which deals with the study of reptiles and amphibians such as snakes, turtles, lizards, tortoises, crocodilians etc. It deals with their behaviour, geographic ranges, physiologies, development, genetics etc. Ornithology – Ornithology is a branch of zoology that deals with the study of birds. Dermatology – A branch of medicine concerned with the diagnosis and treatment of skin, nails and hair diseases. Trophology – Study of nutrition. (i.e., a branch of science dealing with nutrition). Cell (कोशिका) biology – Cell biology is a branch of biology that studies the structure and function of the cell, which is the basic unit of life. Histology – The branch of biology dealing with the study of the microscopic structure of tissues. Embryology – A branch of biology concerned with the study of embryos and their development. Phycology or Algology – A branch of biology which deals with the scientific study of seaweeds and other algae (शैवाल). Mycology – A branch of biology which deals with the scientific study of fungi. Lichenology – It is the study of lichens, symbiotic organisms made up of an intimate symbiotic association of a microscopic alga (or a cyanobacterium) with a filamentous fungus (कवक) (mycobiont). Main Terminologies and related by-products Apiculture – The raising and care of bees for honey and other hive products and agricultural purposes (pollination services, including cross pollination). Aviculture – The breeding and rearing of birds. Aquaculture – The farming of aquatic organisms–fish, molluscs, crustaceans and aquatic plants–for food and other commercial/public purposes, often with intervention to enhance production. Algaculture – A form of aquaculture involving the farming of algae. Algae can be used in wastewater treatment and nutrient recovery; harvested algal biomass can be used as fertilizer (उर्वरक). Phytoplankton are free-floating microscopic algae (often referred to as microalgae) and, in many classifications, include cyanobacteria in sunlit waters. Pisciculture – The controlled breeding and rearing of fishes. Agriculture – The science or practice of farming, including cultivation of the soil for the growing of crops and the rearing of animals to provide food, wool and other products. Sericul ture – The production of silk and the rearing of silkworms for this purpose. Silviculture – Silviculture is the practice of controlling the growth, composition, health and quality of forests to meet diverse needs and values. Spongiculture – The cultivation and growing of (marine) sponges. Horticulture – Horticulture is the science and art of growing fruits, vegetables, flowers and crops like spices/condiments and other plantation crops. Floriculture – Floriculture or flower farming, is a discipline of horticulture concerned with the cultivation of flowering and ornamental plants for gardens and for floristry, comprising the floral industry. Olericulture – Olericulture is the science of vegetables growing, dealing with the culture of non-woody (herbaceous) plants for food. Arboriculture – Cultivation and management of trees and shrubs. Pomology – The science that deals with fruit and fruit growing. Agronomy – A branch of agriculture dealing with crop production and soil management. Vermiculture – The cultivation of earthworms, especially in order to use them to convert organic waste into fertilizer (vermicompost). Oyster culture – The breeding and farming of oysters (primarily for food). Pearls are produced through pearl culture using pearl oysters. Heliculture – It is also known as snail farming. It is the process of raising land snails especially for human use, either to use their flesh to eat or to obtain snail slime for use in cosmetics and snail eggs for human consumption as a type of caviar. Viticulture – Cultivation and harvesting of grapes. Mariculture – Mariculture is a specialized branch of aqua- culture involving the cultivation of marine organisms (animal and plant) for food and other products. Moriculture – Moriculture is the science of mulberry cultivation for silk production, because silkworm (Bombyx mori) is reared on mulberry leaves. Citriculture – Cultivation of citrus fruit trees. Vegeculture – Agricultural systems producing mainly vegetatively propagated root and tuber crops; crops are reproduced asexually by planting parts of the parent plant. Aeroponics – A plant-cultivation technique without soil, in which the roots hang suspended in the air while nutrient solution is delivered to them in the form of a fine mist. Aeroculture – A method of growing plants without soil by suspending them above sprays that constantly moisten the roots with water and nutrients. Hydroponics – It is the cultivation of plants in nutrient enriched water, with or without the mechanical support of an inert medium (e.g. soil).
  • Earth formed around 4.54 billion years ago, by accretion from the solar nebula; this is about one third of the Universe’s current age.
  • Several hypotheses have been proposed regarding the origin of life on Earth. The ‘Oparin–Haldane Hypothesis’ is a foundational early framework for chemical evolution (जैव विकास), but the detailed pathway of abiogenesis is not settled into a single universally accepted model. Oparin-Haldane Hypothesis of Origin of Life :
  • According to this hypothesis, life originated on early Earth through physico chemical processes: atoms combined to form molecules; these reacted to produce simple inorganic and organic compounds; organic compounds then gave rise to macromolecules and increasingly organized systems that eventually led to the first living system or cells.

Oncology is the branch of medical science that deals in treating people suffering from cancer.Teratology is the study of abnormalities of physiological development. Ornithology is a branch of zoology that is related to the study of birds.

Evolution (जैव विकास) of Life: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Earth formed around 4.54 billion years ago, by accretion from the solar nebula; this is about one third of the Universe’s current age.
  • Several hypotheses have been proposed regarding the origin of life on Earth. The ‘Oparin–Haldane Hypothesis’ is a foundational early framework for chemical evolution (जैव विकास), but the detailed pathway of abiogenesis is not settled into a single universally accepted model. Oparin-Haldane Hypothesis of Origin of Life :
  • According to this hypothesis, life originated on early Earth through physico chemical processes: atoms combined to form molecules; these reacted to produce simple inorganic and organic compounds; organic compounds then gave rise to macromolecules and increasingly organized systems that eventually led to the first living system or cells.
  • Hydroponics is a subset of hydroculture and is a method of growing plants using mineral nutrient solutions in water without soil. It is a technique of growing plants in a nutrient solution rich in essential elements/minerals; sometimes inert media such as sand, perlite, or gravel are used for root support. This method is also called soilless cultur...
  • Osteology – Study of bones Orology – Study of mountains Serology – Study of serum Geology – Study of internal structure of the Earth and matter found inside it
  • Primitive Earth's atmosphere was rich in methane, ammonia, water vapour, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, nitrogen, hydrogen, neon etc. It was a reducing atmosphere, with no free oxygen. Evolution (जैव विकास) of Life: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Thus, according to this hypothesis ‘life’ originated upon our Earth spontaneously from non-living matter. First inorganic compounds and then organic compounds were formed in accordance with ever-changing environmental conditions. The hypothesis places ‘origin of life’ within chemical evolution (जैव विकास) operating under early-Earth conditions that differed from present conditions.
  • Hypothesis given by Oparin about origin of life is also known as Oparin’s chemical-evolution (जैव विकास) / ‘primordial soup’ formulation; influenced by dialectical materialism which was published in his book 'Origin of Life'.
  • Modern discussions treat origin-of-life studies as involving prebiotic chemical evolution (जैव विकास) and subsequent biological evolution, with multiple competing hypotheses for key steps. A. Chemical Evolution (Chemogeny) :
  • Among the given options, blue-green algae (शैवाल) is the oldest organism on Earth. They obtain their energy (ऊर्जा) through photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण). They are also known as cyanobacteria. By producing gaseous oxygen as a by-product of photosy- nthesis, they played a greater role in dramatically changing life forms on Earth by stimulating biodiversity.
  • Hugo De Vries is known for his mutation (उत्परिवर्तन) theory of evolution (जैव विकास). According to him, new species are not formed by continuous variations but by the sudden appearance of variations, which he named as mutations. He stated that mutations are heritable and persist in successive generations.
  • The Mesozoic Era is the age of the dinosaurs and lasted almost 180 million years from approximately 250 to 65 million years ago. This era includes three well known periods, called the Triassic, Jurassic and Cretaceous periods.

  3. Animal cells and plant cells both contain cell membrane, cytoplasm, and nucleus. The nucleus has two major functions. It stores the cell’s h...RO/ARO Mains ’131 paper

Cell (कोशिका): Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • The cell (कोशिका) (from Latin Cella– meaning 'small room') is the basic structural, functional and biological unit (मात्रक) of all known living organisms.
  • A cell (कोशिका) is the smallest unit (मात्रक) of life. Cells are often called the ‘building blocks’ of life.
  • The study of cells is called cell (कोशिका) biology or cellular biology.
  • The cell (कोशिका) consists of cytoplasm enclosed within a plasma (प्लाज्मा) membrane. Cytoplasm contains many biomolecules such as proteins and nucleic acids, while the plasma membrane is mainly lipids and proteins.
  • The number of cells in plants and animals varies from species to species. Adult human of 70 kg contain around 30 trillion (3 × 1013) cells.
  • Most plant and animal cells are visible only under a microscope, with dimensions between 1 to 100 micrometres.
  • Cells were discovered by Robert Hooke in 1665 (detailed description in a book written by him– Micrographia).
  • Cell (कोशिका) theory, first developed in 1839, by Schleiden and Schwann. According to their theory, all living organisms are composed of one or more cells and the cell is the most basic unit (मात्रक) of life. Virchow (1855) added that all new cells arise only from pre-existing cells.
  • Mycorrhiza is a symbiotic association formed between certain fungi and the roots of higher plants. It helps the plant-especially by increasing the absorption of phosphorus, nitrogen, and other micronutrients. Plants with mycorrhiza also get protection from root-borne diseases and from some harmful soil microorganisms.
  • Lichens are sensitive to atmospheric pollution (especially sulphur dioxide, etc.); therefore, they are widely used as reliable bioindicators of air quality. Generally, in polluted areas (especially where SO 2 is high), lichens do not grow well. Their diversity/abundance is higher in clean air, where- as in highly polluted areas they may even be abs...

Cell (कोशिका): Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Mitochondria act as the powerhouses, it means that the maximum energy (ऊर्जा) in form of ATP is produced within the mitochondria, hence it is also known as Powerhouses of the cell (कोशिका).
  • During cellular respiration (श्वसन), the initial process takes place in cytoplasm and then in mitochondria.
  • The most prominent roles of mitochondria are to produce the energy (ऊर्जा) currency of the cell (कोशिका), ATP (adenosine triphosphate), through respiration (श्वसन), and to regulate cellular metabolism. During aerobic respiration, most ATP is formed during oxidative phosphorylation. (f) Golgi body :
  • Golgi body is also known as Golgi complex or Golgi apparatus. It first observed in 1897 and officially reported in April, 1898 by Camillo Golgi.
  • The Golgi complex is responsible for processing/ modifying, sorting and packaging certain cellular products such as proteins and lipids that are synthesized by the cell (कोशिका). It consists of stacks of flattened sacs called cisternae. (g) Lysosomes :
  • Lysosomes are also known as the suicide bags of the cell (कोशिका).
  • These are membrane-enclosed organelles that contain an array of enzymes capable of breaking down all types of biological polymers-proteins, nucleic acids, carbohydrates and lipids. It functions as the digestive system of the cell (कोशिका) serving both to obsolete material taken up from outside the cell and to digest obsolete components of the cell itself.
  • Lysosomes also take part in killing the viruses and bacteria (जीवाणु) which have entered within the cell (कोशिका).
  • A plant cell (कोशिका) is different from animal cell mainly on the basis of the cell wall. The outer shell in a plant cell (which is made from cellulose in green plants) is known as cell wall, which is not found in the animal cell. Except this, green plastids are also found in plant cells while not in animal cells.
  • Animal cells and plant cells both contain cell (कोशिका) membrane, cytoplasm, and nucleus. The nucleus has two major functions. It stores the cell’s hereditary material or DNA and it co-ordinates the cell’s activities within cells. Heredity

Comprehensive short notes on Heredity (Biology) with key concepts, definitions, and real UPPSC previous year questions.

Heredity (आनुवंशिकता): Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Heredity (आनुवंशिकता) is the transmission of traits and characteristics via genes/DNA from parents to their offspring.
  • The offspring get their features, characteristics and genetic information from their mother and father.
  • Heredity (आनुवंशिकता) explains why offspring resemble parents, while environment also influences traits.
  • Genetics is a branch of biology that studies the genes, genetic variations and inheritance in living organisms.
  • During the sexual reproduction process, genetic variations occur due to crossing over, independent assortment/ random segregation, and mutations due to errors in DNA replication. Variations are important because they contribute to evolution (जैव विकास) and provide raw material for natural selection.
  • Variations are caused due to gene (जीन) mutations, the interaction of genes with the environment and various combinations of genetic material.
  • Variation can occur through asexual reproduction process too, but these variations are not very noticeable. Gregor Johann Mendel (The Father of Genetics) :
  • Acquiring characteristics or traits from one generation to the other is nothing but inheritance.
  • The cell (कोशिका) membrane (also known as plasma (प्लाज्मा) membrane) is a biological membrane that separates the interior of cell from the outside environment. It is composed of two layers of phospholipids and embedded with proteins.
  • Hybridization was experimented on garden pea plant first by Gregor Mendel. After studying the seven types of qualities of pea, he rendered three laws as– Law of Dominance, Law of Segregation and Law of Independent Assortment.

Heredity (आनुवंशिकता): Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Law of Independent Assortment Genome :
  • A genome is an organism’s complete set of DNA, including all of its genes. Each genome contains all of the information needed to build and maintain that organism. In humans, one copy/haploid set is approx. 3 billion base (क्षार) pairs; most human somatic cells are diploid with two copies in the nucleus, and the human genome also includes mitochondrial DNA; almost every cell (कोशिका) contains a complete copy.
  • Gene (जीन) Mapping : Gene Mapping describes the methods used to identify the locus of a gene and the distance between genes. The essence of all genome mapping is to place a collection of molecular markers onto their respective positions on the genome. Transposable Elements (TEs) :
  • Transposable elements or transposon, also known as jumping genes, are DNA sequences that move from one location on the genome to others. Sometimes TEs create or reverse mutations and alter the cell (कोशिका)'s genetic identity and genome size. These elements were first identified by geneticist Barbara McClintock and for this discovery, she was awarded Nobel Prize of Medicine in 1983.
  • The production of spotted seeds in maize is due to the jumping genes.
  • Almost half of our DNA sequences are made of jumping genes. Most are inactive; only a small fraction can still mobilize, including in germline/early development contexts. They are important to evolution (जैव विकास). But their mobilization can also cause new mutations that lead to diseases such as haemophilia and cancer. Nucleic Acid (अम्ल) :
  • The term nucleic acid (अम्ल) is the overall name for DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) and RNA (ribonucleic acid). They are composed of nucleotides which are the monomers made of three components : a 5-carbon sugar, a phosphate group and a nitrogenous base (क्षार).
  • Nitrogenous bases are of two types– (i) Purine (ii) Pyrimidine.
  • A gene (जीन) is the basic physical and functional unit (मात्रक) of heredity (आनुवंशिकता). Change in the base (क्षार) sequence within a gene is called mutation (उत्परिवर्तन). Mutation is the changing of the structure of a gene, resulting in a variant form that may be transmitted to the subsequent generations. Mutation is the ultimate source of all genetic variation, providing the raw material on wh...
  • The cell (कोशिका) nucleus is a membrane-bound organelle found in eukaryotic cells, acting as the control center and repository of genetic information. The cell nucleus, containing the cell's genetic material (DNA), plays a crucial role in cell reproduction by housing and replicating the DNA, which is then distributed to daughter cells during processes like ...

Heredity (आनुवंशिकता): Pathology, Traps & High-Yield PYQs

  • It carries hereditary information from parents to offspring. Human Chromosomes :
  • Human is unisexual animal. In human, each cell (कोशिका) normally contains 23 pairs of chromosomes, for a total of 46. Twenty-two of these pairs called autosomes in males and females alike. The 23rd pair, the sex chromosomes, differ between males and females.
  • In males, the chromosomes are 44 + XY (i.e., 46, XY) and in females, chromosomes are 44 + XX (i.e., 46, XX).
  • At conception, the mother contributes an X chromosome (गुणसूत्र) and the father contributes either X or Y . XX usually results in a female and XY usually results in a male (exceptions can occur in some differences of sex development/ aneuploidy). Some terms about heredity (आनुवंशिकता)
  • Gene (जीन) : It is the basic unit (मात्रक) of inheritance. It consists of a sequence of DNA in most organisms; RNA is genetic material in some viruses. The genes can mutate and can take two or more alternative forms.
  • Chromosomes : These are thread-like structures of nucleic acids and protein that are found in the nucleus of eukaryotic cells (prokaryotes lack a membrane-bound nucleus). They carry the heredity (आनुवंशिकता) or genetic information in the form of genes. These are made up of nucleoprotein. German anatomist Waldeyer is known for naming the chromosome (गुणसूत्र). The number of chromosomes varies from species to species but it remains constant for a particular species. In most animals, however, any change from the typical chromosome number for a species may be due to genetic disorders.
  • Genotype : It is the complete heritable genetic identity of an organism. It is the actual set up of alleles that are carried by the organisms.
  • Phenotype : It is the description of the observable characteristics including internal/functional traits of an organism, the way the genotype is expressed.
  • Friedrich Miescher first identified and isolated (discovered) DNA in 1869. Double helix model of DNA was given by James Watson and Francis Crick in 1953.
  • Tyrosine or 4-hydroxyphenylalanine is one of the 22 amino acids that are used by cells to synthesize proteins. Its codons are UAC and UAU. It is a non-essential amino acid (अम्ल) with a polar side group. It was first discovered in 1846 by German chemist Justus von Liebig in the protein casein from cheese. It is called tyrosyl when referred to as a functio...

Biofertilizers: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Over the years, chemical fertilisers have helped farmers increase crop production to meet rising demand. However, excessive and continuous use of chemical fertilisers can cause water pollution and may reduce soil fertility by harming soil microorganisms and by not replenishing organic matter in the soil. Hence, there is a growing push towards organic and other sustainable farming approaches, including the use of biofertilizers.
  • Biofertilizers are beneficial microorganisms that enrich the nutrient quality of the soil and improve nutrient availability to plants. They are used to replenish soil nutrients and reduce dependence on chemical fertilisers, thereby helping in more environment-friendly crop production. Types of Biofertilizers :
  • Following are the types of biofertilizers–
  • Biofertilizers are products that contain living or dormant beneficial microorganisms (such as Rhizobium, Azotobacter, Azospirillum, phosphate-solubilizing Bacillus, etc.) which, when applied to seeds/soil, enhance nutrient availability. Organic manures or green manures are nutrient sources but are not biofertilizers.
  • Biofertilizers are defined as preparations containing living cells or latent cells of efficient strains of microorganisms that help crop plants uptake of nutrients, by their interactions in the rhizosphere when applied through seed or soil. They accelerate certain microbial processes in the soil which aug- ment the extent of availability of nutrien...

  • Azolla has the capability of nitrogen-fixing. That is why it is being widely used as a biofertilizer (जैव उर्वरक), especially in parts of Southeast Asia.

Skeletal and Muscle System: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Anatomy is the branch of biology concerned with the shape, structure and relationship of parts of the organism.
  • The skeletal system forms the rigid internal framework of the body in vertebrates, including humans. Together, the muscular system and skeletal system are known as the musculoskeletal system.
  • The skeletal system is composed of the bones, cartilages and ligaments (and associated tissues) that perform essential functions of the body.
  • Bone tissue or Osseous tissue is the major structural and supportive connective tissue of the body. It forms the rigid part of the bones that make up the skeletal system. Bone tissue is different from bones themselves– bones are organs containing multiple tissue types (e.g., blood, connective tissue, nerves, and bone tissue) and also contain bone marrow, blood vessels, and nerves; the outer surface is covered by the fibrous periosteum. It helps in movement and protects the internal organs of the animals.
  • Cartilages, a resilient and semirigid connective tissue found on the skeleton in areas of flexibility and smooth surface support movement.
  • Ligaments are the fibrous and dense connective tissues, that connect bones to other bones.
  • The amount of water in the human body generally ranges from 55-70%. The average adult human body have about 55-65% water. The percentage of water in infants is much higher typically around 75-78%, dropping to 65-70% by one year of age. Water is the best solvent in nature.
  • Nearly 99% of the mass (द्रव्यमान) of human body consists of just six chemical elements– oxygen, carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, calcium and phosphorus. Oxygen is the most abundant element (तत्व) in the human body. It is mainly found in the form of water. Water, makes up generally about 55-70% of the human body and participates in countless metabolic reactions.
  • Almost 99% of the mass (द्रव्यमान) of the human body is made up of six elements, these are approximately : Oxygen (65%), Carbon (18.5%), Hydrogen (9.5%), Nitrogen (3.2%), Calcium (1.5%) and Phosphorus (1.0%). So according to question, option (d) is correct.
  • Calcium phosphate is the principal form of calcium found in bovine milk and blood. About 70% of bone consists of hydroxyapatite, a calcium phosphate mineral (known as bone mineral). Up to 50% by volume and about 70% by weight (भार) of human bones is a modified form of hydroxyapatite.

Skeletal and Muscle System: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • A tendon (or sinew) is a fibrous connective tissue that attaches muscle to bone (tendons may also attach muscles to other structures such as the eyeball) is designed to withstand tension.
  • The major functions of the skeletal system are body support, facilitation of movement, protection of the internal organs, storage of mineral and fat, and blood cell (कोशिका) formation.
  • Bones serve as a site for fat storage and blood cell (कोशिका) production. The unique semi-solid tissue that fills the interior of most bones is referred to as bone marrow.
  • There are two types of bone marrow : (i) Yellow bone marrow which contains adipose tissue and triglycerides stored in the adipocytes of this tissue can be released to serve as a source of energy (ऊर्जा) for other tissues of the body and (ii) Red bone marrow, where the production of blood cells (named hematopoiesis, hemato = "blood", poiesis = "to make") takes place. Red blood cells, white blood cells and platelets (प्लेटलेट्स/पट्टिकाणु) are all produced in the red bone marrow.
  • In humans, the skeleton has been divided into two major parts : Digestion (पाचन) :
  • Digestion (पाचन) is the breakdown of large insoluble food molecules into small water-soluble food molecules so that they can be absorbed into the watery blood plasma (प्लाज्मा). Digestion is a form of catabolism that is often divided into two processes based on how food is broken down : mechanical and chemical digestion.
  • The number of bones in an adult human body is 206. The body of a child has about 300 bones at birth, which decreases to 206 bones by adulthood after some bones have fused together. Together these bones form the axial skeleton and the appendicular skeleton.
  • The stapes is the lightest stirrup-shaped bone and the smallest bone in the human body found in the middle of humans ears. While femur is the largest bone in the human body. It is located in the upper leg which connects the knee at one end and fits into the hip socket at the other.
  • The joint where the neck joins the head is a pivot (pivotal) joint, also known as a rotary joint, allowing for head rotation and some flexion and extension. A pivot joint is a type of synovial joint where a cylindrical bone rotates within a ring-shaped bone, allowing for rotational movement along a single axis.

The alimentary canal is a long tube through which food is taken into the body and digested.

  • Saliva is the watery and usually somewhat frothy substance produced in the mouth by salivary glands.
  • Human saliva comprises about 99.5% water plus electrolytes, mucus, white blood cells, epithelial cells (from which DNA can be extracted), enzymes such as amylase (ptyalin) and lipase, antimicrobial agents such as secretory IgA, and lysozymes.

It is made of hard compact bone that is resistant to bending. The inner cavity of the diaphysis contains yellow bone marrow that stores fat. The epiphysis is a thin shell of compact bone filled wi...
  • Hip arthroplasty or hip-joint replacement surgery involves replacing components of the hip-joint with a synthetic implant, to repair the damaged part that is causing pain.
  • A ligament is a tissue that connects two bones to form a joint. It is fibrous and dense connective tissue.
  • Dental plaque is a biofilm or mass (द्रव्यमान) of bacteria (जीवाणु) that grows on surfaces within the mouth. It appears as a white/pale yellow 'slime layer' commonly found in between the teeth and along the cervical margins . Skeletal and Muscle System: Advanced Classifications & Observations

  • The amount of saliva that is produced in a healthy person per day ranges from about 0.75 to 1.5 litres.
  • The digestive functions of saliva include moistening food, and helping to create a food bolus, so it can be swallowed easily.
  • Amylase enzyme (एंजाइम) (ptyalin) breaks some starches into maltose and dextrin.

  • Oesophagus or esophagus is also called the gullet or food pipe.
  • It is a muscular tube about 25 cm. long. It connects mouth to the stomach (आमाशय).
  • Alpha-keratin is a family of fibrous structural proteins. It is the key structural material making up the outer (epidermis) layer of human skin. It is the primary component of hair, nails, hooves, and it provides the necessary strength and toughness for masticatory organs, such as the tongue and the hard palate.
  • Hair is composed of the protein keratin, which is also the primary component of finger and toe nails.
  • Keratin is an extremely strong protein that is a major component in skin, hair, nails, hooves, horns and teeth.
  • Teeth and bones both are hard, white and heavy. Teeth are composed of calcium, phosphorous and other minerals. Bones contain calcium, phosphorus, sodium and other minerals, but mostly consist of the protein collagen.

Digestive System: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Digestion (पाचन) is the breakdown of large insoluble food molecules into small water-soluble food molecules so that they can be absorbed into the watery blood plasma (प्लाज्मा). Digestion is a form of catabolism that is often divided into two processes based on how food is broken down : mechanical and chemical digestion.
  • Mechanical digestion (पाचन) refers to the physical breakdown of large pieces of food into smaller pieces which can subsequently be accessed by digestive enzymes.
  • In chemical digestion (पाचन), enzymes break down food into the small molecules the body can use. Human digestive system :
  • The human digestive system consists of the alimentary canal (gastrointestinal tract) plus the accessory organs of digestion (पाचन) (the tongue, salivary glands, pancreas (अग्न्याशय), liver (यकृत) and gall bladder). The alimentary canal is a long tube through which food is taken into the body and digested.
  • In human, this passage is about 30 feet (9 metre) long from the oesophagus to the anus. The buccopharyngeal cavity, oesophagus, stomach (आमाशय) and intestines are the main parts of the alimentary canal. Digestion (पाचन) in Mouth :
  • Saliva is the watery and usually somewhat frothy substance produced in the mouth by salivary glands.
  • Human saliva comprises about 99.5% water plus electrolytes, mucus, white blood cells, epithelial cells (from which DNA can be extracted), enzymes such as amylase (ptyalin) and lipase, antimicrobial agents such as secretory IgA, and lysozymes.
  • The amount of saliva that is produced in a healthy person per day ranges from about 0.75 to 1.5 litres.
  • The strongest muscle based on its weight (भार) is the masseter, known as jaw muscle. With all muscles of the jaw working together, it can close the teeth with a force (बल) as great as about 55 pounds (25 kilograms) on the incisors or about 200 pounds (90.7 kilograms) on the molars.
  • Digestion (पाचन) begins in the mouth when we chew and swallow and is completed in the small intestine (आंत). The small intestine is a long tube loosely coiled in the abdomen (spread out, it would be more than 20 feet long). The small intestine continues the process of breaking down food by using enzymes released by the pancreas (अग्न्याशय) and bile from the liver (यकृत).

Digestive System: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • The pancreas (अग्न्याशय) is an organ of the digestive system and endocrine (अंतःस्रावी) system of vertebrates. It is both an endocrine gland and exocrine (बहिःस्रावी) gland. Hence it is called the mixed gland. In adults, it is about 12-15 cm long, lobulated and salmon-coloured in appearance.
  • It is the second one of the largest gland in human.
  • In human, it is located in the abdomen behind the stomach (आमाशय).
  • As an endocrine (अंतःस्रावी) gland, it functions mostly to regulate blood sugar levels, secreting the hormones insulin, glucagon and somatostatin, and pancreatic polypeptide. Pancreatic juice :
  • Pancreatic juice is a liquid secreted by the pancreas (अग्न्याशय), which contains a variety of enzymes.
  • The enzymes present in pancreatic juice are as follows : (i) Amylase– It converts starch into disaccharides. (ii) Trypsin– It converts protein into smaller peptides which are then further hydrolized into amino acids. (iii) Lipase– It converts fat into glycerol and fatty acids. (iv) Elastase– It break down proteins. (v) Nuclease– It breaks down the nucleic acid (अम्ल) into nucleotide.
  • Since all types of food are digested by pancreatic juice, so it is called complete digestive juice. Digestion (पाचन) of food in duodenum takes place in alkaline medium Small Intestine (आंत) :
  • The small intestine (आंत) is a long, narrow, folded or coiled tube extending from the stomach (आमाशय) to the large intestine; it is the region where most digestion (पाचन) and absorption of food takes place.
  • In human body, the digestion (पाचन) of protein begins in the stomach (आमाशय) with the action of enzyme (एंजाइम) pepsin. Pepsin is secreted by the gastric glands of the stomach which digests the protein into smaller peptides and amino acids, which can be readily absorbed by the small intestine (आंत).
  • The chief/peptic cells of the stomach (आमाशय) secrete a pro-enzyme (एंजाइम) called pepsinogen, which gets activated in the presence of hydrochloric acid (अम्ल) (HCl) and becomes pepsin. This pepsin initiates protein digestion (पाचन) in the stomach. In contrast, trypsin and chymotrypsin are secreted by the pancreas (अग्न्याशय) in inactive forms (trypsinogen/chymotrypsinogen), and their activ...

Digestive System: Pathology, Traps & High-Yield PYQs

  • Enzyme (एंजाइम) is a substance produced by a living organism which acts as a catalyst (उत्प्रेरक) to bring about a specific biochemical reaction.
  • Zymase enzyme (एंजाइम) converts glucose into ethyl alcohol and carbon dioxide. It occurs naturally in yeasts. Food :
  • Food is any substance consumed to provide nutritional support for an organism.
  • It is usually of plant or animal origin and contains essential nutrients such as carbohydrates, fats, proteins, vitamins and minerals.
  • The substance is ingested by an organism and assimilated by the organism’s cell (कोशिका) to provide energy (ऊर्जा), maintain life or stimulate growth. Components of Food :
  • The major components of our food are- (i) Carbohydrates (ii) Fats (iii) Proteins (iv) Minerals (salts) (v) Nucleic Acids (vi) Vitamins (vii) Water Carbohydrates :
  • A carbohydrate is a biomolecule consisting of carbon (C), hydrogen (H) and oxygen (O) atoms, usually with a hydrogen - oxygen atom (परमाणु) ratio of 2 : 1 and thus with the empirical formula Cx(H2O)y (Where x may or may not be different from y).
  • Carbohydrates are the main component of food and source of immediate energy (ऊर्जा) to the body.
  • The correctly matched lists are as follows : Vitamin (विटामिन) – Carotene Enzyme (एंजाइम) – Pepsin Hormone (हार्मोन) – Testosterone/Progesterone Protein – Keratin
  • The liver (यकृत) is the largest gland of the human body. Extra glucose in the body gets converted into glycogen and is stored in the liver. Glucose is a simple sugar found in carbohydrates. Once carbohydrates are absorbed from food, they are carried to the liver for processing.

Vitamins and Nutrition: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Food is any substance consumed to provide nutritional support for an organism.
  • It is usually of plant or animal origin and contains essential nutrients such as carbohydrates, fats, proteins, vitamins and minerals.
  • The substance is ingested by an organism and assimilated by the organism’s cell (कोशिका) to provide energy (ऊर्जा), maintain life or stimulate growth. Components of Food :
  • The major components of our food are- (i) Carbohydrates (ii) Fats (iii) Proteins (iv) Minerals (salts) (v) Nucleic Acids (vi) Vitamins (vii) Water Carbohydrates :
  • A carbohydrate is a biomolecule consisting of carbon (C), hydrogen (H) and oxygen (O) atoms, usually with a hydrogen - oxygen atom (परमाणु) ratio of 2 : 1 and thus with the empirical formula Cx(H2O)y (Where x may or may not be different from y).
  • Carbohydrates are the main component of food and source of immediate energy (ऊर्जा) to the body.
  • Approximately 55-75% energy (ऊर्जा) is provided by carbohydrates to the total energy requirement of the body.
  • It is stored in the liver (यकृत) and skeletal muscles in the form of glycogen which gives energy (ऊर्जा) on requirement.
  • The term 'nutraceutical' is used to describe any food or part of food supplements that offers a medical or health benefit beyond simple nutrition. Such benefits many include the prevention or recurrence of the diseases.
  • Kazimierz (Casimir) Funk is generally credited with being among the first to formulate the concept of vitamins, which he called 'vital amines' or 'vitamins'. Umetaro Suzuki had in 1910 succeeded in extracting a water-soluble complex of micronutrients from rice bran and had named it 'aberic acid (अम्ल)'.

Respiratory System: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • There are three major parts of the respiratory system : the airway, the lungs (फेफड़े) and the muscles of respiration (श्वसन).
  • The airway, which includes the nose, mouth, pharynx, larynx, trachea, bronchi and bronchioles, carries air between the lungs (फेफड़े) and body's exterior.
  • Human have two lungs (फेफड़े), a right lung and a left lung. They are situated in the thoracic cavity of the chest. The right lung is bigger than the left, which shares space in the chest with the heart (हृदय). The right lung has three lobes and the left has two.
  • The lungs (फेफड़े) act as the functional units of the respiratory system. Their function in the respiratory system is to extract oxygen from the atmosphere and transfer it into the bloodstream and to release carbon dioxide from the bloodstream into the atmosphere, in a process of gas exchange.
  • Air is breathed in through the nose or the mouth. In the nasal cavity, a layer of mucous membrane acts as a filter and traps pollutants and other harmful substances found in the air. Next, the air is moved into the pharynx (also known as the throat), a passage that contains the intersection between the oesophagus and the larynx. The opening of the larynx has a special flap of elastic cartilage, the epiglottis, that opens to allow air to pass through but closes to prevent food from moving into the airway.
  • From the pharynx, air moves into the trachea (or windpipe) and down to the intersection that branches to form the right and left primary bronchi . Each of these bronchi branches into secondary (lobar) bronchi, that branches into tertiary (segmental) bronchi and that split into many smaller airways called bronchioles, that eventually connect with tiny specialized structures called alveoli (approx. 15 crore in each lung) that function in gas exchange.
  • The lungs (फेफड़े) are encased in a serous membrane that folds in on itself to form the pleurae, a two-layered protective barrier. The inner visceral pleura covers the surface of the lungs and the outer parietal pleura is attached to the inner surface of the thoracic cavity. The pleurae enclose a cavity called the pleural cavity that contains pleural fluid. This fluid is used to decrease the amount of friction that lungs experience during breathing.
  • At the base (क्षार) of the lungs (फेफड़े) is a sheet of skeletal muscle called the diaphragm. The diaphragm is the main muscle of respiration (श्वसन) involved in breathing and is controlled by the sympathetic nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र). When the diaphragm contracts, it moves inferiorly a few inches into the abdominal cavity, expanding the space within the thoracic cavity and pulling air into the lungs. Relaxation of the diaphragm allows air to flow back out the lungs during exhalation.
  • Cholesterol belongs to the steroid family of lipid (fat) compounds. It is a sterol (or modified steroid) – an unsaturated alcohol of the steroid family of compounds. It is fatty alcohol found in animal fat. It is essential for the normal function of all animal cells and is a fundamental element (तत्व) of their cell (कोशिका) membranes. It is also a precursor of var...
  • The correct matching is as follows: Mineral oil – Petroleum Vegetable fats – Phytosterol Mustard oil – Lubricant Animal fats – Cholesterol Mustard oil is not used as a lubricant. Hence, option (c) is the incorrectly matched pair. IV . Respiratory System Notes • There are three major parts of the respiratory system : the airway, the lungs (फेफड़े) and the mu...

Respiratory System: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • In anaerobic respiration (श्वसन), glucose breaks down without oxygen. Incomplete oxidation (ऑक्सीकरण) of glucose takes place producing alcohol and carbon dioxide. C 6H12O6 → 2C2H5OH + 2CO2 + 56Kcal energy (ऊर्जा).
  • Such type of respiration (श्वसन) is found in resting seeds, pericarps of fruits, microorganisms and muscles of animals.
  • The formation of lactic acid (अम्ल) in the muscle cell (कोशिका) is also an example of anaerobic respiration (श्वसन).
  • Fermentation is a type of anaerobic respiration (श्वसन). b. Aerobic Respiration :
  • Aerobic Respiration (श्वसन) is a biochemical reaction which takes place in the presence of oxygen.
  • The by-products of aerobic respiration (श्वसन) are carbon dioxide and water. C 6H12O6 + 6O2 → 6CO2 + 6H2O + 656Kcal energy (ऊर्जा).
  • Anaerobic respiration (श्वसन) (both glycolysis and fermentation) takes place in the fluid portion of the cytoplasm whereas the bulk of the energy (ऊर्जा) yield of aerobic respiration takes place in mitochondria.
  • Aerobic respiration (श्वसन) involves two stages - (i) Glycolysis (ii) Kreb's or Citric Cycle. (i) Glycolysis :
  • Hypoxemia or low level of oxygen in the blood describes a lower than normal level of oxygen in the blood. In order to function properly, our body needs a certain level of oxygen circulating in the blood to cells and tissues. When this level of oxygen falls below a certain amount, hypoxemia occurs and you may experience shortness of breath. In other...
  • Adenosine triphosphate (ATP) is the high energy (ऊर्जा) molecule (अणु) that stores the energy. The conversion of ATP to ADP is an extremely crucial reaction for the supplying of energy for life processes. ATP hydrolysis is the final link between the energy derived from food or sunlight and useful work (कार्य) such as muscle contraction, the establishment of electrochemi...

Circulatory System

Circulatory System: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • The circulatory system is a network consisting of blood, blood vessels and the heart (हृदय). This network supplies tissues in the body with oxygen and other nutrients, transport hormones and removes unnecessary waste products.
  • There are two main types of circulatory systems : open circulatory systems and closed circulatory systems.
  • Open circulatory systems are systems where internal organs and body tissues are surrounded by circulatory fluid, e.g. in crustaceans, insects, molluscs and other invertebrates.
  • Closed circulatory systems have the blood closed at all times within vessels of different size and wall thickness. In this type of system, blood is pumped by a heart (हृदय) through vessels and does not normally fill body cavities. Large and more active animals, including all vertebrates, have a closed circulatory system. Human Heart :
  • The human heart (हृदय) is a muscular organ, which pumps blood throughout the body via the blood vessels of the circulatory system, supplying oxygen and nutrients to the tissues and removing carbon dioxide and other wastes.
  • In human, the heart (हृदय) is roughly the size of a large fist and weighs between about 250-300 grams in adults.
  • The human heart (हृदय) is located between the lungs (फेफड़े), in the middle compartment (mediastinum) of the chest.
  • The Pericardium is a double-walled sac enclosing the heart (हृदय), consisting of an outer fibrous membrane and an inner double layer of serous membrane. The intervening space between these layers is filled with pericardial fluid which protects the heart from outer shocks and acts as a lubricant to allow normal heart movement within the chest.
  • Each 'heartbeat' consists of a contraction and relaxing of the heart (हृदय) muscles. When the heart contracts, it pumps blood through the blood vessels. When it relaxes, it draws blood in. It takes rest between relaxation and contraction.
  • A human heart (हृदय) has two upper and two lower chambers (total four chambers). The upper chambers, the right and left atria, receive incoming blood. The lower chambers, the more muscular right and left ventricles, pump blood out of the heart. The heart valves, which keep blood flowing in the right direction, are gates at the chamber openings.

Circulatory System: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • High blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) is when blood pressure is persistently higher than normal.
  • Blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) that's high over a long time is one of the main risk factors for heart (हृदय) disease. As humans get older, the chances of having persistently high blood pressure increases.
  • It is very important to get blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) checked regularly, and if it is persistently high it needs to be controlled.
  • Uncontrolled high blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) can lead to a heart (हृदय) attack or stroke. It may attack kidneys.
  • The medical name for high blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) over a long period of time is hypertension.
  • The causes of high blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) are as follows-
  • eating patterns including salty foods
  • The sino-atrial node often abbreviated SA-node is the pacemaker of the heart (हृदय) and is responsible for the initiation of the heart beat. The sino-atrial node is located in the right atrium (right upper chamber) of the heart.
  • The pulmonary veins are the only veins in the body that carry oxygenated blood. They carry oxygenated blood from the lungs (फेफड़े) to the heart (हृदय)'s left atrium. All other veins carry deoxygenated or oxygen-poor blood. Similarly, pulmonary arteries are the only arteries in the body that carry oxygen- poor blood. All the rest of arteries carry oxygenated blood...
  • Blood is a special connective tissue consisting of a fluid matrix, plasma (प्लाज्मा), and formed elements. The total volume of blood in a 70 kg normal adult human being is 5-6 litres.

Circulatory System: Pathology, Traps & High-Yield PYQs

  • It is a fluid, a specialized form of connective tissue.
  • Its pH is about 7.4 (alkaline solution).
  • The amount of blood in the human body is generally equivalent to about 7% of body weight (भार). Its average density (घनत्व) around 1060 kg/m3. The average adult has a blood volume of roughly 5-6 litres.
  • Blood is essential to life. It circulates through the body and delivers essential substances like oxygen and nutrients to the body's cells. It also transports metabolic waste products away from those cells. There is no substitute for blood. It cannot be made or manufactured. Generous blood donors are the only source of blood for patients in need of a blood transfusion. Components of the blood :
  • The main components of the blood are – (A) Plasma (प्लाज्मा) (B) Blood cells or corpuscles (A) Plasma :
  • Plasma (प्लाज्मा) is the relatively clear, yellow tinted water (about 91-92%), sugar, fat, protein and salt (लवण) solution which carries the blood corpuscles.
  • Normally about 55% of our blood volume is made of plasma (प्लाज्मा).
  • Plasma (प्लाज्मा) also contains blood clotting factors, vitamins, enzymes, hormones, antibodies and other proteins. (B) Blood corpuscles :
  • Red blood cells (RBCs) or red blood corpuscles also called erythrocytes are the most common type of blood cell (कोशिका) and the vertebrate organism’s principal means of delivering oxygen (O2) to the body tissues–via blood flow through the circulatory system. RBCs take up oxygen in the lungs (फेफड़े) or gills and release it into tissues while squeezing through the bo...
  • Myoglobin (Mb) is an oxygen-binding heme protein found in the skeletal muscle tissue of vertebrates in general and in almost all mammals. It contains iron (Fe). It is distantly related to haemoglobin. Compared to haemoglobin, myoglobin has a higher affinity for oxygen and does not have cooperative binding with oxygen like haemoglobin does.

  • Excretion (उत्सर्जन) is a process by which metabolic waste is eliminated from an organism.

  • This is in contrast with secretion, where the substance may have specific tasks after leaving the cell (कोशिका).

Excretory System: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Excretion (उत्सर्जन) is a process by which metabolic waste is eliminated from an organism.
  • In vertebrates, this is primarily carried out by the lungs (फेफड़े), kidneys and skin.
  • This is in contrast with secretion, where the substance may have specific tasks after leaving the cell (कोशिका).
  • Excretion (उत्सर्जन) is an essential process in all forms of life. Classification of animals on the type of nitrogenous waste :
  • The animals have been classified into three groups on the basis of the type of nitrogenous waste : (a) Ammonotelic animals : Their main nitrogenous waste is ammonia e.g. certain protozoans, polychaete annelids, crustacean arthropods, molluscs (aplysia, sepia and octopus), fresh-water teleost fishes, tadpoles of amphibians and crocodiles. (b) Ureotelic animals : These animal’s main nitrogenous waste is urea e.g. elasmobranch fishes, amphibian and mammals. (c) Uricotelic animals : Their main nitrogenous waste is uric acid (अम्ल) e.g. insects, some gastropods, lizards, snakes and birds. Excretion (उत्सर्जन) of nitrogenous waste in human :
  • The nitrogenous waste in human is excreted by kidneys.
  • The kidneys are bean-shaped organs which are present on each side of the vertebral column in the abdominal cavity.
  • Humans have two kidneys and each is supplied with blood from the renal artery.
  • Scientists from the University of Sheffield, Britain are developing an artificial ‘plastic blood’, which could act as a substitute for real blood in emergency situations. The ‘plastic blood’ have a huge impact on military applications in war zones. Because the artificial blood is made from a plastic, it is light (प्रकाश) to carry, does not need to be kept c...
  • The liver (यकृत) is responsible for the detoxification of alcohol. Liver detoxify harmful substances through the complex chemical reactions.

Excretory System: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • The urine is acidic with pH 6.0. Dialyzer or Artificial Kidney (वृक्क/गुर्दा)
  • A dialyzer is an artificial filter containing fine fibres. The fibres are hollow with microscopic pores in the wall, also known as a semipermeable membrane.
  • Its function is to remove the excess waste and fluid from the blood when the patient's kidneys can no longer perform that task.
  • The process of purifying blood in body is known as dialysis.
  • Its pale yellow colour is due to the presence of pigment urochrome (urobilin).
  • In urine, water about 91-96%; urea- about 2.6%; ions of unnecessary salts-about 2%; cretanin-about 0.3% and very small amount of uric acid (अम्ल) and other waste materials in trace amount is found.
  • The kidneys secrete a variety of hormones, including erythropoietin, calcitriol and renin.
  • Most alcohol is broken down or metabolized by an enzyme (एंजाइम) by liver (यकृत) cells known as alcohol dehydrogenase. It breaks down alcohol into acetaldehyde and then another enzyme aldehyde dehydrogenase, rapidly breaks down acetaldehyde into acetate.
  • Kidneys remove excess organic molecules from the blood and it is by this action that their best-known function is performed–the removal of waste products of metabolism. They serve the body as a natural filter of the blood and remove water-soluble wastes, such as urea and ammonium and they are also responsible for the reabsorption of water, glucose ...
  • The process, ultrafiltration occurs at the barrier between the blood and the filtrate in the renal capsule in the kidney (वृक्क/गुर्दा). The kidneys remove urea and other toxic wastes from the blood, forming a dilute solution called urine in the process.

Nervous System (तंत्रिका तंत्र): Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • The nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र) is a complex collection of nerves and specialized cells known as neurons that transmit signals between different parts of the body. It is essentially the body's electrical wiring.
  • In other words, we can say that a nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र) is a group of organized cells specialized in the conduction (चालन) of electrochemical stimuli from sensory receptors through a network to the site at which a response occurs.
  • A neuron or nerve cell (कोशिका) is a cell that carries electrical impulses.
  • Neurons are the basic unit (मात्रक) of the nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र).
  • Every neuron is made of a cell (कोशिका) body (also called a soma), dendrites and a single axon.
  • Dendrites and axons are nerve fibres.
  • Nerve cells are the longest cell (कोशिका) of the body.
  • A neuron is an electrically excitable cell (कोशिका) that communicates with another cell via specialized connections called synapses.
  • A kidney (वृक्क/गुर्दा) stone is a hard object that is made from chemicals in the urine. There are four types of kidney stones : calcium oxalate, uric acid (अम्ल), struvite, and crystine. The most common type of kidney stone which is created when calcium combines with oxalate in the urine. Oxalate is one type of substance that can form crystals in the urine. This can ha...
  • The brain (मस्तिष्क) is composed of the cerebrum, cerebellum, and brainstem. The cerebrum is the largest part of the brain and it performs higher functions like interpreting touch, vision and hearing as well as speech, reasoning, learning and fine control of movement. The function of the cerebellum is to coordinate muscle movements, maintain posture and balan...

Nervous System (तंत्रिका तंत्र): Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Many organs are controlled primarily by either the sympathetic or the parasympathetic division. Sometimes the two divisions have opposite effects on the same organs. For example - the sympathetic division increases blood pressure (रक्तचाप) (दाब) and the parasympathetic division decreases it. Overall, the two divisions work (कार्य) together to ensure that the body responds appropriately to different situations.
  • The two chemical messengers (neurotransmitters) are used to communicate within the autonomic nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र) :
  • Norepinephrine (Noradrenaline)
  • Nerve fibres that secrete acetylcholine are called cholinergic fibres and which secrete norepinephrine are called adrenergic fibres.
  • Generally, acetylcholine has parasympathetic (inhibiting) effects and norepinephrine has sympathetic (stimulating) effects. However, acetylcholine has some sympathetic effects too. For example - it sometimes stimulates sweating or makes the hair stand. (c) Enteric nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र) :
  • It consists of a mesh-like system of neurons that governs the function of the gastrointestinal tract. It is capable of acting independently of the sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र), although it may be influenced by them. It is also called the second brain (मस्तिष्क).
  • There is no pain or tactile (touch) receptors of any type in the brain (मस्तिष्क) itself. So, a needle or even more invasive brain surgery is not associated with any ‘sensation’ of the brain. Brain does not have any pain receptors.
  • The Cerebellum is often called the ‘Little Brain (मस्तिष्क)’ because of its shape, structure, and micro-anatomical similarities. It is located in the cranial cavity just below the cerebral hemi - spheres and behind the pons-medulla. Main functions of Cerebellum are as follows : • Bodily balance and coordination • Regulation of muscle tone and precise movement...
  • ‘Satiety’ and ‘thirst’ centres in humans are located in the hypothalamus of the brain (मस्तिष्क). This area of the brain governs body temperature (तापमान), thirst, hunger, sleep, circadian rhythm, moods, sex drive and the release of various hormones in the body.
  • The temperature (तापमान) of the human body neither decreases in winters nor increases in summers. The usual temperature of the human body is 98.6°F or 37°C and is controlled by the hypothalamus in the brain (मस्तिष्क). The hypothalamus functions as a type of thermostat for the body.

  • Asexual reproduction is not limited to single-celled organisms. The cloning of an organism is a form of asexual reproduction.

Reproduction and Embryo Development: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Reproduction is the biological process by which new individual organisms – ‘offspring’ – are produced from their parents.
  • Reproduction is a fundamental feature of all known life ; each individual organism exists as the result of reproduction.
  • There are two forms of reproduction : asexual and sexual.
  • In asexual reproduction, an organism can reproduce without the involvement of another organism.
  • By asexual reproduction, an organism creates a genetically similar or identical copy of itself.
  • Asexual reproduction is not limited to single-celled organisms. The cloning of an organism is a form of asexual reproduction.
  • Sexual reproduction typically requires the sexual interaction of two specialized organisms, called gametes, which contains half the number of chromosomes of normal cells and are formed by meiosis, with typically a male fertilizing a female of the same species to create a fertilized zygote.
  • This produces offspring organisms whose genetic characteristics are derived from those of the two parental organisms. Human Reproduction :
  • Melatonin is a hormone (हार्मोन) secreted by the pineal gland in the brain (मस्तिष्क). It helps to regulate other hormones and maintains the body’s circadian rhythm. The circadian rhythm is an internal 24-hour ‘clock’ that plays a critical role at the time when we fall asleep and when we wake up. When it is dark, our body produces more melatonin. When it is bright, the...

Reproduction and Embryo Development: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Prenatal stages and development include germinal, embryonic and fetal period.
  • The first two weeks of development is the germinal period, which begins with conception and ends when the blastocyst is fully implanted into uterine tissue.
  • Embryonic period of prenatal development lasts from implantation until about 8 weeks from the time of conception (or the 10th week of pregnancy). This is the most important time of prenatal development because the embryo is developing the foundations for a healthy baby.
  • Three germ layers are formed in this period – The outer layer is called the ectoderm, the middle layer is called the mesoderm and inner layer is called the endoderm.
  • Each germ layer is differentiated into different structures. The ectoderm forms many outer tissues such as skin, hair, nervous system (तंत्रिका तंत्र) tissues including brain (मस्तिष्क). The mesoderm forms tissues inside the body such as lungs (फेफड़े), bones and muscles including the heart (हृदय). The endoderm forms tissues such as the digestive tract and bladder as well as other internal organs.
  • Fetal period : Once cell (कोशिका) differentiation is mostly complete, the embryo enters the fetal period and becomes known as a fetus. This period of development begins during the ninth week and lasts until birth. In this period the formed organs are mature and growth takes place. The heart (हृदय) of fetus starts to beat in 4th-5th week of its development.
  • Amnion : The amnion is a membrane that closely covers the embryo when it first formed. It is filled with amniotic fluid, which causes the amnion to expand and become the amniotic sac, which serves to provide a protective environment for the developing embryo or fetus.
  • The amnion along with chorion, the yolk sac and the allantois form a protective sac around the embryo.
  • Pheromones are chemical substances produced and released into the environment by an animal especially insect, affecting the behavior or physiology of others of its species.
  • A sperm remains alive for up to about 72 hours after entering into oviduct but its capacity to fertilize the ovum lasts for about 48 hours (2 days).

Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण): Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • The process of photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is one of the most remarkable activities of green plants.
  • The autotrophic plants synthesize enormous amounts of organic food with the help of the light (प्रकाश) energy (ऊर्जा) available from the sun.
  • Carbohydrates produced through photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) constitute the basic raw materials, which directly or indirectly give rise to all the organic components of virtually all plants and animals.
  • The entire humanity depends upon the prepared food of plants.
  • It is one of the most massive biochemical event going on the earth.
  • Most plants, most algae (शैवाल), and cyanobacteria perform photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण); such organisms are called photoautotrophs.
  • Approximately 90% of the world's photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is carried out by marine and freshwater algae (शैवाल).
  • Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is the process of synthesis of carbohydrate from carbon dioxide and water in presence of sunlight and chlorophyll.
  • Magnesium is present in chlorophyll. Green plants are dependent on chlorophyll for photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) and magnesium is required for chlorophyll production. In one of the crucial step in photosynthesis, light (प्रकाश) absorbed by chlorophyll excites electrons in the molecules, enabling them to be transferred to other molecules. Magnesium deficiency manifest itse...
  • Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is that process of the plant in which light (प्रकाश) energy (ऊर्जा) is converted into chemical energy and stored in food in the form of carbohydrates. The free glucose produced by photosynthesis usually quickly gets converted into complex sugars such as starch or sucrose and is stored in cells and storage organs. Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण): Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Duration of the light (प्रकाश) period has also a significant effect on the total photosynthetic yield of a plant. A plant carries on much photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) if it is given light for 10 to 12 hours per day.
  • The carbon dioxide, oxygen, temperature (तापमान) and water also affect the rate of photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण).
  • Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) occurs in a very wide range of temperatures. Certain conifers can photosynthesize at as low temperature (तापमान) as –35 oC while some algae (शैवाल) photosynthe size at about 75oC. In temperate regions, photosynthesis has a rapid rate between 10oC to 35oC, provided other factors are not limiting. Process of Photosynthesis :
  • Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is a chemical oxidation (ऑक्सीकरण)-reduction (अपचयन) (redox) reaction, in which water is oxidized to release oxygen and carbon dioxide is reduced to form the glucose.
  • Process of photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is completed in two phases– (a) Light (प्रकाश)-dependent phase/Photochemical oxidation (ऑक्सीकरण) of water/Light reactions/Hill reaction :
  • These reactions takes place in grana of the chloroplast.
  • In the light (प्रकाश)-dependent reactions, one molecule (अणु) of the pigment chlorophyll absorbs one photon and loses one electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन).
  • This electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) is passed to a modified form of chlorophyll called pheophytin, which passes the electron to a quinone molecule (अणु), starting the flow of electron down an electron transport chain that leads to the ultimate reduction (अपचयन) of NADP to NADPH.
  • Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) is the process of converting light (प्रकाश) energy (ऊर्जा) to chemical energy and storing it in the bonds of sugar. In this process, carbon dioxide (CO2) is taken in from the atmosphere and oxygen (O 2) is released out during the formation of glucose (C6H12O6). Chemical Reaction :
  • Approximately 90% of the photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) on Earth is done by aquatic plants and algae (शैवाल). Among these, 85% is in the sea (mainly by algae) and remaining 5% is in river, ponds etc. In the process of photosynthesis, CO 2 (carbon dioxide) is absorbed and carbohydrate and oxygen are produced.

Photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण): Pathology, Traps & High-Yield PYQs

  • Every organism is an open system linked to its environment by a continuous exchange of energy (ऊर्जा) and materials.
  • In ecosystems, plants and other photosynthetic autotrophs perform the crucial step of transforming inorganic compounds into organic ones.
  • Plants need sunlight as the energy (ऊर्जा) source for photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण).
  • They also need inorganic raw materials such as water, carbon dioxide (CO 2) and inorganic ions to synthesize organic molecules.
  • Plants obtain CO2 from the air. CO 2 diffuses into leaves from the surrounding air through stomata.
  • Most vascular plants obtain water and minerals from the soil through their roots.
  • Plants require nine macronutrients and at least eight micronutrients to sustain their life.
  • Elements required by plants in relatively large quantities are called macronutrients.
  • The process of photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) takes place only in the visible part (3800 - 7600 wavelengths) of the electromagnetic radiations. Best photosynthesis takes place in the red light (प्रकाश) and next best in blue light while in green light, it is nill. T.W. Englemann (1888), had shown the action spectrum of photosynthesis in his experiment, in which algae (शैवाल) in red...
  • In the process of photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण), light (प्रकाश) energy (ऊर्जा) changes into the chemical energy. Normally plants utilize sunlight but marine algae (शैवाल) also use moonlight. Photosynthesis even occurs in electric light.

Plant Nutrition: Structure, Anatomy & Functions

  • Every organism is an open system linked to its environment by a continuous exchange of energy (ऊर्जा) and materials.
  • In ecosystems, plants and other photosynthetic autotrophs perform the crucial step of transforming inorganic compounds into organic ones.

  • They also need inorganic raw materials such as water, carbon dioxide (CO 2) and inorganic ions to synthesize organic molecules.
  • Plants obtain CO2 from the air. CO 2 diffuses into leaves from the surrounding air through stomata.
  • Most vascular plants obtain water and minerals from the soil through their roots.

Plant Nutrition: Physiological Processes & Mechanisms

  • Plants can increase phosphorus uptake by a mutualism with mycorrhiza. A phosphorous deficiency in plants is characterized by an intense green coloration or reddening in leaves due to lack of chlorophyll.
  • Phosphorus deficiency can produce symptoms similar to those of nitrogen deficiency but it differs in being extremely difficult to diagnose. (vi) Potassium (K)– Potassium regulates the opening and closing of stomata by a potassium ion pump.
  • Potassium deficiency may cause necrosis or interveinal chlorosis. It may also result in higher risk of pathogens, wilting, brown spotting and higher chances of damage from frost and heat (ऊष्मा). (vii) Sulphur (S)– Sulphur is a structural component of some amino acids (including cysteine and methionine) and vitamins.
  • It is essential for chloroplast growth and functions.
  • It is found in the iron-sulphur complex of the electron (इलेक्ट्रॉन) transport chains in photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण).
  • It is needed for N2 fixation by legumes.
  • Symptoms of deficiency include yellowing of leaves and stunted growth. (viii) Calcium (Ca)– Calcium regulates transport of other nutrients into the plant and is also involved in the activation of certain plant enzymes. It also involved in photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण) and plant structure.
  • Calcium deficiency results in stunting. Blossom end rot is also a result of inadequate calcium. (ix) Magnesium (Mg)– The outstanding role of magnesium in plant nutrition is as a constituent of the chlorophyll molecule (अणु).
  • Xylem is composed of dead cells, while phloem is made up of living cells. Phloem transports food from the leaves to the rest of the plant body.
  • Essential elements are classified into following two categories: (i) Macroelements (Major elements) – These are required by the plant in larger quantities e.g. Carbon (C), Hydrogen (H), Oxygen (O), Nitrogen (N), Phosphorus (P), Potassium (K), Magnesium (Mg), Calcium (Ca) and Sulphur (S). (ii) Microelements (Minor elements or Trace elements) – These...

Plant Nutrition: Pathology, Traps & High-Yield PYQs

  • Khaira disease of rice plant (Paddy plant) is due to the deficiency of zinc. (vii) Nickel (Ni)– In higher (vascular) plants, nickel is absorbed by plants in the form of Ni2+.
  • Nickel is essential for activation of urease, an enzyme (एंजाइम) involved with nitrogen metabolism that is required to process urea. Without nickel, toxic levels of urea accumulate, leading to the formation of necrotic lesions.
  • In lower (non-vascular) plants, nickel activates several enzymes involved in a variety of processes. (viii) Chlorine (Cl)– Chlorine as compounded chloride, is necessary for osmosis and ionic balance. It also plays a role in photosynthesis (प्रकाश संश्लेषण). Vascular System :
  • The separation between plants that have veins and plants that do not is one of the great division within the plant kingdom.
  • This separates plants into vascular (higher) and non- vascular (lower) plants.
  • Most plants have xylem & phloem and are known as vascular plants but some more simple plants such as algae (शैवाल) and mosses (bryophyta), do not have xylem or phloem and are known as non-vascular plants.
  • Xylem : Xylem tissue is also known as water-conducting tissue.
  • Xylem is for transporting water and minerals absorbed by the roots.
  • Nitrogen is one of the most essential nutrients for plant growth. Although nitrogen gas in the atmosphere represents the largest quantity of the nutrient in the environment, it is unavailable to most plants as a direct source. Nitrogen is normally absorbed by plants from soil in the form of nitrate (NO3–) ions, although in acid (अम्ल) environments such as...
  • Boron is an essential micronutrient for plants. It is essential for development and growth of new cells in plant meristem. It is necessary for 'Nodule Formation' in legumes. It is associated with translocation of sugars, starches, nitrogen and phosphorus.
